 1   Q33.   WHY DID THE COMPANY ASSUME THAT SMALL PROJECTS WOULD MOVE FROM 50% TO 25%

 2          IN SOME OF THE STEPS?

 3   A33.   As the Company did not provide any information about its assumptions, it was unclear to

 4          what the 50% and 25% applies. Does this mean that only 25% of projects are reviewed, have

 5          contracts generated, undergo witness testing, or have the meter reprogrammed? Are project

 6          review and generate agreement different steps? Do all projects that undergo witness testing

 7          also have their meter reprogrammed, or do some projects require meter reprogramming but

 8          not witness testing? None of this is clear in the Company’s analysis.

 9                  While we were unable to ask discovery to find out these answers, we were able to ask

10          these questions during an informal Q&A session with the Company. The Company clarified

11          it is able to increasingly automate simple “cookie-cutter” projects under 10 kW, avoiding the

12          need of a manual review of basic project information (e.g., the account number matching the

13          address, system components are UL listed, etc.) and witness testing, and are able to have

14          semi-automated interconnection agreements created. The Company also clarified that all

15          meters undergo field reprogramming, and all projects must have a contract generated, despite

16          only listing that 50% to 25% of systems under 10 kW incur these costs.

17   Q34.   WHY DID THE COMPANY ASSUME THAT A CONSTANT 3,555 PROJECTS UNDERGO THE

18          PROJECT BILLING AND TARIFF RIDER ADDED STEP?

19   A34.   This was confirmed during our conversation to be an error. The 3,555 projects represented

20          the total number of systems that were brought online in 2021, the baseline year for the

21          analysis. This value was inadvertently applied to 2023-2025 installations rather than using

22          the projected number of projects for those years.

23   Q35.   WHY DID THE COMPANY FAIL TO INCLUDE A WITNESS TESTING COST PER APP FOR SYSTEMS

24          OVER 250 KW?

25   A35.   [Mr. Lucas] I initially assumed this to be an error, as it is directly contradicted by the

26          Company’s proposed changes to the Rules and Regulations, which clearly states: “3. All

27          customer generation sized 250 kilowatts and above, whether installed behind-the-meter or


                                                                                                          19

 1              stand alone, must undergo a system impact study and the witness testing process as required

 2              by the Company and will be responsible for all associated fees.” 32

 3                       However, the Company confirmed during our conversation that not only do all

 4              systems over 250 kW undergo witness testing, but also that this witness testing was

 5              sufficiently complex and expensive that the Company decided to exclude these costs from the

 6              common interconnection fee calculation.

 7   Q36.       IS THIS DECISION IN ANYWAY CONSISTENT WITH ITS APPROACH FOR THE REST OF THE

8               COSTS?

 9   A36.       No, it is not, and it shows the arbitrary nature of the Company’s proposal. The Company is

10              perfectly content to require systems under 10 kW to subsidize $2,000-$2,500 studies and

11              $2,100 project reviews for systems over 250 kW, but it drew a line at the witness testing fee

12              and required large projects to directly pay this fee. Why is it ok to exclude an expensive

13              witness test from common cost recovery, but ok to include expensive study and project

14              review costs in common cost recovery? Further, this confirmation directly undermines its

15              statement that the $200 fee “collects the known costs directly attributed to process project

16              applications for interconnection” as the mandatory witness testing fee for systems over 250

17              kW is specifically excluded from the calculation.

18   Q37.       ARE THERE OTHER UNEXPLAINED VARIATIONS IN THE WORKPAPER?

19   A37.       [Mr. Lucas] Yes. The number of projects that go through the various study process is

20              inconsistent with the number of projects listed. Table 2 below shows the total count of

21              projects by size, along with the number of studies conducted.

 2           It appears from these figures that most but not all projects over 10 kW are required to

 3   have a Feasibility Study, but there is no explanation for why 5, 4, and 5 of the projects over

 4   10 kW in 2023, 2024, and 2025, respectively would not require a Feasibility Study.

 5   Likewise, it seems that the Standard Impact Study applies to projects over 250 kW but

 6   appears that some projects between 10 and 250 kW also require this study. Only a handful of

 7   projects require a Network Underground or Reliability Study; again, no context was provided

 8   for these assumptions.

 9           I was able to get some answers regarding this step during my conversation with the

10   Company. GPC explained that all projects, including projects under 10 kW, undergo a basic

11   screening process that checks to make sure the account information is correct, and that the

12   system size does not exceed the local transformer size. Projects that pass this initial screen –

13   which includes an increasing number of small projects – do not need further studies. At the

14   same time, the Company performs a Feasibility Study on 100% of the projects over 10 kW

15   and a handful of projects under 10 kW that fail the initial screen. Likewise, the Impact Study

16   is mandatory for 100% of systems over 250 kW and the few projects that do not pass the

17   initial Feasibility Study process.

18           The Network Underground is only done on systems that intend to connect to an urban

19   mesh grid and require additional operational analysis, such as ensuring the system does not

20   export to the grid. The Reliability Study is triggered for the few projects that use new


                                                                                                   21

 1              equipment that has not been encountered by the Company before, even if the equipment is

2               UL listed and conforms with IEEE standards. According to the Company, this is primarily

3               solar plus storage projects.

 6   Q44.       WERE YOU ABLE TO GET MORE INFORMATION ABOUT THE BILLING MODIFICATION PROCESS

 7              IN YOUR INFORMAL CONVERSATION?

 8   A44.       [Mr. Lucas] Yes, I was, and the answers were very surprising. Based on my understanding of

 9              the conversation, the steps need to process billing system changes is extremely inefficient,

10              requiring multiple “touches” of the customer’s account throughout the interconnection

11              process. As I recall, the Company said that billing personnel had to make account

12              adjustments to the billing system three different times in the process. This is followed by

13              manual review of every single RNR account to ensure that the billing system, meter reads,

14              and netting calculations are performed correctly.

15   Q45.       DOES GPC PROVIDE ANY SUPPORT FOR THE COST OF EACH STUDY OR STEP IN THE

16              INTERCONNECTION REVIEW PROCESS?

17   A45.       [Mr. Lucas] No, it did not. The studies range from $53 for the Feasibility Study to $2,472 for

18              the Network Underground study. Similarly, the Project Review and Generate Agreement

19              costs vary substantially; $105 for systems under 10 kW, $157 for systems between 10 and

20              250 kW, and $2,096 for systems over 250 kW. No data, such as the number of labor hours

21              required to perform the study, is provided to support these figures.

22                       The Company assumes a cost of $105 for systems under 10 kW to complete the

23              project review and generate the interconnection agreement. Given the Company’s use of

24              PowerClerk, which standardizes the intake of project information, it should be trivial to

25              automatically generate a standard form interconnection agreement. However, I learned

26              during my meeting that PowerClerk cannot directly create the interconnection agreement

27              despite having all the relevant information. Instead, a GPC employee must enter the


     40
          Hearing transcript page 1094 lines 5-14


                                                                                                               25

 1          information into another form, which can then be used to generate an interconnection

 2          agreement. It is unclear why this functionality is not fully automated.

 3                  The Company also assumes a total cost of $88 for the Project Billing and Tariff Rider

 4          Added step. Given that GPC has not indicated anywhere in its testimony that it manually

 5          bills these customers, I expected RNR bills to be generated automatically by the billing

 6          system software and that enabling an RNR tariff would involve little more than updating a

 7          few account flags in the Company’s billing system. But after my conversation with the

 9   Q46.   PLEASE EXPAND ON THIS POINT.

10   A46.   [Mr. Lucas] I am particularly concerned about the revelation that billing analysts have to

11          manually verify every account to ensure the billing system is working properly. The details

12          were a bit unclear, but it was suggested that this step was needed to validate that the tariff

13          was eligible for the RNR program, that meter reads were properly pulled into the system, that

14          solar generation was properly read, that the netting calculation was done correctly.

15                  It is concerning how a billing system that surely cost millions of dollars and an AMI

16          infrastructure that cost tens or hundreds of millions of dollars needs to be manually checked

17          for such basic billing functionality. Before I switched to clean energy policy, I worked for

18          Accenture for six years implementing enterprise resource software at Fortune 500 companies.

19          When I worked on these systems 20 years ago, I would never have designed a process that

20          needed this level of manual intervention, nor would any client ever accept such a solution as

21          it would be notoriously inefficient and fail to achieve adequate cost savings.


22     Under the Company’s Proposal, Customers with Small Systems Would Subsidize Customers with

23                                               Larger Systems

24   Q47.   DOES THE COMPANY PROPOSE TO CHARGE INDIVIDUAL PROJECTS BASED ON THE STUDIES

25          AND PROCESS STEPS THEY ARE REQUIRED TO PERFORM?


                                                                                                             26

 1   A47.   No, it does not. Rather, it proposes to charge every project a pro rata share of the total cost of

 2          all the studies for all projects. For example, the Feasibility Study (at a cost of $53 per study

 3          in 2023) is generally constrained to projects over 10 kW, and the Standard Impact ($1,988),

 4          Network Underground ($2,472), and Reliability ($227) studies are applicable to just a

 5          handful of projects over 250 kWAC. Rather than charge these costs to the size category, the

 6          Company proposes to have every system pay $9 in 2023 for these studies.

 7                  Similarly, the Company assumes that 50% of projects under 10 kW will undergo the

 8          Project Review and Generate Agreement step (at $105 per application in 2023), while all

 9          projects between 10 and 250 kW ($157 per application) and over 250 kW ($2,096) will

10          require this step. As with the interconnection study, rather than charge the costs on a per-size

11          basis, the Company lumps all costs into one average $59 fee for this step. This means that a

12          large project saves more than $2,000 by having smaller projects subsidize its project review.

13   Q48.   WHAT WAS THE COMPANY’S JUSTIFICATION FOR THIS APPROACH?

14   A48.   Mr. Legg discussed the Company’s approach during cross-examination “So I think the

15          thought was, yeah, it's the -- these costs are spread across all of these different customers.

16          There may be some that vary by size of customer, but generally these are costs that are being

17          caused across, regardless of size, across this group of customers and they should be

18          responsible for paying those costs.”

19   Q49.   IS THIS STATEMENT SUPPORTED BY THE EVIDENCE PRESENTED IN THE DISCOVERY

20          RESPONSE?

21   A49.   [Mr. Lucas] No, it is not. I was able to produce an analysis that broke out the various costs

22          from the Company’s workpaper on a per application basis. This required making

23          assumptions about the cost of the excluded witness test fee for systems over 250 kW and to

24          correct the error related to the 3,555 projects in the Project Billing and Tariff Rider Added

25          step. I assumed the Witness Test for systems over 250 kW cost $2,500 in 2023 as this was

26          just slightly higher than the highest cost that the Company deemed appropriate to socialize

27          among all projects.

 7   Q52.    DID THE COMPANY PROVIDE ANY EVIDENCE REGARDING THE PREVALENCE OF FLAT

 8           INTERCONNECTION FEES REGARDLESS OF SYSTEM SIZE?

11           aware.” 41

12   Q53.    DID YOU REVIEW THE INTERCONNECTION FEES FOR UTILITIES IN NEARBY STATES?

13   A53.    Yes. Two themes appeared from this review, neither of which are found in the Company’s

14           proposal. First, utilities charged differential fees based on system size. Second, small

15           systems often pay no interconnection fees.

16                    The state of Florida issued regulations guiding the interconnection process. 42 These

17           rules establish three tiers: under 10 kW, between 10 and 100 kW and between 100 kW and 2

18           MW. For the smallest tier, utilities are prohibited from charging any interconnection fees,

19           and for the two larger tiers, the utility can petition the Florida commission to implement cost-

20           based fees. Florida Power and Light charges a flat $400 fee for projects between 10 and 100

21           kW. 43 For projects between 100 kW and 2 MW, FPL charges an application fee of $1,000

22           and, if necessary, and interconnection study fee of $2,000. 44

10           PROPOSAL?

11   A54.    [Mr. Lucas] The Company has offered no evidence in its testimony or exhibits that the

12           current fees fail to collect the costs for reviewing interconnection applications. The

13           Company’s witness at the hearing was unable to explain what each step consisted of, much

14           less whether the values in the Company’s workpaper were supportable. He also contradicted

15           key elements of the Company’s own documentation, which clearly shows that

16           interconnection fees are highly dependent on system size. And despite this, GPC proposes to

17           dramatically increase fees for small systems. Under the current regime, a customer with a 7-

18           kW system would pay $35. This would increase to $200 under its proposal.

20           unsupported assumptions, and at least one calculation error. The number of projects is

21           inconsistently handled within the workpaper, and no justification for these discrepancies was

22           provided. Some of the steps are performed less frequently in future years, reducing costs, but

 3          discovery responses. Steps that appear to be quite basic – such as flagging an account as a

 4          RNR customer in the billing system – have costs that imply unreasonably high levels of

 5          effort. Although I was able to clarify some of these issues – including the surprisingly

 6          inefficient billing process – during my meeting with the Company, it has offered no evidence

 7          into the record on these points that support its cost structure.

 8                  Even if the Company was able to demonstrate that it is under-collecting

 9          interconnection review costs, its proposal is unfair to small customers. Customers installing

10          larger systems require impact and feasibility studies that are simply not performed for small

11          systems. In total, the cost of the interconnection review for a project over 250 kW is more

12          than 40 times more expensive than for a project under 10 kW, and yet the Company proposes

13          charging every customer the same fee.

14                  Further, the Company arbitrarily decided to socialize high study costs for large

15          systems while selectively choosing to exclude witness testing fees for those same projects.

16          This approach is completely inconsistent with its stated claim that all projects should bear

17          interconnection costs proportionately.

18   Q55.   WHAT IS YOUR RECOMMENDATION ON THIS ISSUE?

19   A55.   We recommend the Commission reject the proposal and maintain its current fees of $5/kW

20          for systems under 250 kW. If the Company is able to demonstrate that its costs for routine

21          steps such as billing system updates and project review are reasonable, and that the current

22          fees under-collect interconnection review costs, the Commission should direct the Company

23          to establish tiered interconnection fees that seek to minimize costs for systems under 10 kW.

24          The interconnection fees of neighboring utilities discussed above provide the Commission

25          with a range of reasonable options.


                                                                                                           31

 1   IV.        GEORGIA POWER’S NET METERING REVENUE ADJUSTMENT IS FLAWED AND

 2              LACKS CONTEXT

 3   Q56.       WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF THIS SECTION OF YOUR TESTIMONY?

 4   A56.       We discuss the Company’s proposed $1.4 million revenue adjustment related to “base

 5              revenue erosion associated with the monthly netting cost shift.” 50 Given the Company plans

 6              to provide RNR-related testimony in the second phase of this proceeding, we limit our

 7              discussion to the specific $1.4 million figure and its incorporation into the Company’s cost of

 8              service study. We anticipate the Company will produce more than the one paragraph of

 9              testimony it submitted in this round – half of which was directly copied from its IRP filing –

10              in the second phase of this case, and we will discuss its broader RNR testimony at the

11              appropriate time.

12   Q57.       PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR FINDINGS.

13   A57.       The $1.4 million adjustment is based solely on lost revenue and not supported by any cost of

14              service analysis. The Company did not incorporate any demand or energy reductions from

15              exported energy from RNR customers in its cost of service study (“COSS”) model. This

16              results in the Company using billing determinants that are too high for classes with either

17              RNR Monthly or RNR Instantaneous customers, which in turn results in too many costs

18              being allocated to these classes. These steps are necessary to determine whether the $1.4

19              million in revenue erosion is offset in full or in part by cost reductions from exported energy.

20                       Even if one were to take the $1.4 million base revenue erosion figure at face value, it

21              pales in comparison to the astounding amount of excess revenue that the Company has

22              collected from its customers over the past decade. While the Company touts that the

23              “embedded sharing mechanism” associated with the Alternate Rate Plan structure “has

24              enabled the Company to provide approximately $297 million in benefits back to its

25              customers since 2013”, this is a spectacularly misleading statement. In reality, the Company


     50
          Legg Direct at 11.


                                                                                                              32

 1             has collected more than $1.8 billion above its approved ROE from customers in the past

 2             eleven years, with only a small fraction of that returned to customers through the sharing

 3             mechanism.

 4   Q58.      WHAT IS YOUR RECOMMENDATION WITH REGARD TO THE PURPORTED COST SHIFT?

 5   A58.      We recommend the Commission deny the Company’s request to adjust revenue by $1.4

 6             million. The Company did not appropriately account for exported energy in its COSS, and as

 7             a result, has overallocated costs to customer classes with RNR projects that is not considered

 8             in this figure. This hits the residential class most directly given its higher proportion of

 9             exported energy.

10                     We also recommend the Commission strongly reconsider the width and asymmetry of

11             the currently-approved ROE dead band. The Company has consistently managed its

12             operations to increase its profits well beyond the central approved ROE value. Expanding

13             the delta between the ROE and the upper end of the dead band has resulted in the Company

14             collecting $114 million in 2020 and 2021 that would have otherwise been returned to

15             customers.


16    The Company Does Not Incorporate Demand or Energy Reductions from Exported RNR Generation in

17                                                  its COSS Model

18   Q59.      HOW DOES THE COMPANY CALCULATE THE $1.4 MILLION COST SHIFT FIGURE?

19   A59.      The Company’s original filing did not contain details on the calculation, so Staff asked for

20             the workpapers in discovery. 51 In response, the Company provided a nearly-identical

21             analysis that it presented in its 2022 Integrated Resource Plan proceeding. 52 This analysis

22             takes the following steps to calculate the figure:


     51
          STF-TAI-1-27.
     52
          STF-TAI-1-27 Attachment A.


                                                                                                              33

 1           1. Calculate the bill a customer would have had on the RNR Instantaneous rate, specific to
 2              the customer’s tariff and inclusive of all rider revenues.
 3           2. Calculate the bill a customer received on the RNR Monthly rate, which first nets inflow
 4              from the Company and exported energy on a monthly basis.
 5           3. Add up the difference between these two values for all months for all customers on a
 6              given tariff.
 7           4. Scale each tariffs’ results to 5,000 customers based on the composition of the RNR
 8              active, pending, and wait list customers.
 9   Q60.    IS THIS ARGUMENT BASED ON COST OF SERVICE OR BASED ON REVENUE DEFICIENCY?

10   A60.    This analysis is based entirely on revenue deficiency and does not factor cost of service at all.

11           The Company simply calculated the difference in customer bills on the two netting schemes

12           (instantaneous vs. monthly) and added up the total difference. There is no consideration of

13           how the exported energy impacts the Company’s cost of service.

14   Q61.    DID YOU IDENTIFY AN ISSUE WITH THE AVOIDED SOLAR COST RATE THE COMPANY USED IN

15           ITS ANALYSIS?

16   A61.    Yes. In its workpapers, GPC uses the incorrect solar avoided cost rate. It is using the 2022

17           value of $0.02676/kWh from the 2021 Avoided Cost and Solar Avoided Cost Projection

18           filing in Docket 16573. 53 Nine of the twelve months of data in the Company’s analysis was

19           from 2021, not 2022. GPC should have used the appropriate rate for the appropriate month,

20           or if simplifying the analysis, used an avoided cost figure more weighted towards the 2021

21           value ($0.02884/kWh) than then 2022 value ($0.02676/kWh). Using a lower value for the

22           avoided cost credit rate increases the apparent difference between the monthly and

23           instantaneous RNR netting, making the total “cost shift” appear larger than it would

24           otherwise. While this does not directly impact the base rate calculation as the RNR credit is

25           accounted for separately, it does make the program seem more expensive in total than it

26           should.

27                     We also find it problematic that the Company assumed the solar avoided cost rate

28           will remain constant through the three years of the ARP. This calculation is a function of the

29           Company’s avoided cost of generation, which is itself heavily influenced by the price of

     53
       2021 Avoided Cost and Solar Avoided Cost Projections, Docket 16573, July 30, 2021.
     https://psc.ga.gov/search/facts-document/?documentId=186573


                                                                                                           34

1            natural gas. In fact, when the Company updated its filing this summer, the 2022 BTM solar

 2           avoided cost rate increased from $0.02676/kWh to $0.03400/kWh, a 27% jump. 54 Natural

 3           gas prices have remained high through all of 2022 and are projected to remain elevated

 4           through 2023 as well. 55 This suggests that the solar avoided cost rate will remain higher in

 5           the future than in the past, further inflating the Company’s estimate of the

 6           “cost shift” associated with this program.

 7   Q62.    HOW DOES THE COMPANY TREAT CUSTOMER-GENERATOR ENERGY THAT IS DELIVERED TO

 8           ITS GRID FROM BEHIND-THE-METER (“BTM”) SYSTEMS IN ITS COSS AND ASSOCIATED

 9           WORKPAPERS?

10   A62.    GPC ignores it. The billing determinants that are used in the COSS are not adjusted for

11           excess generation that is exported to the Company’s system.

12   Q63.    HOW DID YOU DETERMINE THIS?

13   A63.    [Mr. Lucas] It was through a combination of discovery responses and an analysis of the

14           Company’s workpapers. GPC provided two data sets in response to Staff discovery requests;

15           one that contained hourly loads during 2021 for the entire Georgia Power system, 56 and one

16           that contained hourly loads during 2021 for each rate schedule. 57 Staff asked subsequent

17           discovery on whether the data file with hourly loads for each rate schedule included exported

18           energy from the RNR tariffs. 58 The Company responded that the original data “does not

19           include customer generated energy (delivered to the Georgia Power Grid) under Rate RNR-

20           10.” 59

21                     I was able to reconcile the data between the schedule-specific hourly load file (which

22           was confirmed to exclude exported energy), the total Georgia Power hourly load file, and the


     54
        2022 Avoided Cost and Solar Avoided Cost Projections, Docket 16573, July 29, 2022.
     https://psc.ga.gov/search/facts-document/?documentId=191003
     55
        https://www.cmegroup.com/markets/energy/natural-gas/natural-gas.quotes.html
     56
        STF-TAI-1-10.
     57
        STF-TAI-1-12.
     58
        STF-TAI-4-2.
     59
        Id.


                                                                                                             35

 1             Company’s COSS model using the historic 2021 test year. 60 In particular, I was able to

 2             confirm that the tariff-specific and total jurisdictional demand figures matched between the

 3             files, meaning they were developed from the same original data source. Given the tariff-level

 4             hourly load file did not contain any excess energy, it follows that the COSS does not either.

 5   Q64.      WHY DOES THIS MATTER?

 6   A64.      Exported energy from a customer’s BTM system flows to meet the nearest demand. In

 7             almost every case for small RNR systems, this will mean the excess energy flows out through

 8             the customer-generators meter to the nearest transformer, and from there flows to the nearest

 9             neighbor’s load. The energy flows through the neighbor’s meter, causing the neighbor to be

10             billed the full retail rate for this energy, including embedded costs for the Company’s entire

11             generation, transmission, and distribution system, as well as rider charges related to fuel

12             expense, nuclear prepayments, and coal ash pond mitigation costs. This is despite the fact

13             that the Company did not generate the energy, does not own the BTM asset, and only a tiny

14             fraction of its distribution system was used to facilitate the energy delivery.

15                       From a COSS perspective, failing to decrement the class’s energy and demand

16             allocators for exported energy means that the Company is overstating the allocators for

17             classes with RNR systems. While the residential class only consumes 36% of electricity

18             purchased from the Company by customers on an RNR tariff, it accounts for nearly 60% of

19             exported energy. 61 These customers invested capital in generators that provide benefits to the

20             Company’s system through peak demand reduction and avoided fuel costs, among other

21             benefits. Further, because residential customers export a relatively higher proportion of

22             generation compared to non-residential customers, they are disproportionately harmed by the

23             Company’s omission of exported generation in the COSS. The Commission should require

24             GPC to incorporate exported energy through the COSS model and flow the resulting class

25             allocator reductions to the appropriate customer class.


     60
          STF-TAI-1-1.
     61
          STF-TAI-4-1.

 1    The $1.4 million NEM Adjustment Pales in Comparison to the Company’s Pending Revenue Requests

 3   Q66.    IS THERE ANOTHER MECHANISM IN THE RATE CASE THAT ALSO EXPOSES CUSTOMERS TO

 4           SIGNIFICANT COST INCREASES?

 5   A66.    Yes. Each rate case, the Commission approves a specific ROE and capital structure.

 6           Currently, the Company enjoys a 10.5% ROE and a 54% 56% equity / 46% 44% debt capital

 7           structure. 68 These values feed into the COSS to calculate the total revenue requirement based

 8           on forecasted net plant in service, operating expenses, and taxes. From this revenue

 9           requirement, rates are designed based on forecasted usage to exactly collect the revenue

10           requirement. Of course, the assumptions embedded in the COSS, load forecast, and cost

11           projections will have errors, and the actual revenues collected and costs incurred will not

12           exactly match those in the COSS models. The result is an ROE that differs from the

13           Commission-approved 10.5%.

14                   This is addressed in Georgia through the use of an ROE earning band mechanism.

15           While rates are calculated based on assumptions to produce an ROE of 10.5%, if the

16           Company collects revenue or manages costs to produce an ROE above or below this figure

17           within a certain range (the “dead band”), no additional action is required. Currently, the

18           Company’s Commission-approved ROE is 10.5%, but the Company’s multi-year rate plan is

19           authorized to operate within an ROE dead band of 9.5% to 12%. 69

20   Q67.    WHAT WAS THE ORIGIN OF THE ROE DEAD BAND MECHANISM?

21   A67.    The ROE dead band concept has been in place since at least the 2010 rate case settlement. In

22           that case, the ROE dead band of 10.25% to 12.25% was established, with a central approved

23           ROE of 11.15%. 70 In the 2013 rate case settlement, which remained in force from 2013

24           through 2018, the Commission approved an ROE dead band of 10% to 12% while dropping


     68
        Order Adopting Settlement Agreement at 6, December 17, 2019, Docket 45216. (“2019 Rate Case Order”)
     69
        2019 Rate Case Order at 9.
     70
        Final Order at 10, December 29, 2010, Docket No. 31958.


                                                                                                              38

 1           the ROE to 10.95%. 71 Two-thirds of earnings in excess of 12% were returned to customers,

 2           and the Company could petition for an interim cost recovery mechanism should the ROE

 3           drop below 10%. 72 In the 2019 rate case settlement, the ROE was reduced to 10.5% and the

 4           ROE dead was adjusted to its current 9.5% to 12.0% with 80% of excess earnings being

 5           returned to customers. 73 GPC agreed to a one-time foregoing of its share of the earnings

 6           above the dead band in 2019 as part of a settlement agreement. 74

 7                   Under this structure, if the ROE obtained from actual adjusted revenues and costs in a

 8           year is between 9.5% and the authorized ROE of 10.5%, the Company would be required to

 9           absorb the lower revenues. Conversely, if actual adjusted revenues and costs produce an

10           ROE between 10.5% and 12%, the Company is entitled to keep all of the excess revenue. If

11           the effective ROE falls below the lower end of the dead band (currently 9.5%), the Company

12           may petition the Commission for an increase in base rates. If the effective ROE rises above

13           the top of the dead ban (currently 12%), the Company must return to customers 80% of

14           earnings above the top ROE threshold.

15   Q68.    ARE APPROVED ROE DEAD BANDS A REASONABLE CONCEPT IN THEORY?

16   A68.    Yes. When implemented properly within a multi-year rate plan construct, an ROE dead band

17           can provide useful flexibility to a utility. Locking in rates for several years increases rate

18           certainty for both the utility and customers and reduces the time and expenses involved in

19           rate cases. Pairing this with an earnings dead band provides flexibility when actual results

20           invariably diverge from forecasts.

21                   However, there must be correspondingly robust controls over the ROE dead band and

22           earnings mechanisms that mitigates extreme outcomes. For example, if rates were calculated

23           based on certain assumptions that were reasonable at the time but turned out to be

24           meaningfully short of what a utility needed to cover its costs and earn a reasonable rate of


     71
        Order Adoption Settlement Agreement, December 23, 2013, Docket 36989.
     72
        Id.
     73
        2019 Rate Case Order at 5-6.
     74
        Id at 11.


                                                                                                              39

 1           return, there would be policy benefits to allowing the Company to request a rate increase

 2           prior to the end of the multi-year plan to help maintain its financial security. On the flip side,

 3           if the embedded assumptions in costs and rates result in the Company earning an outsized

 4           profit, mechanisms should kick in to protect consumers.

 5                   As with all things in utility regulation, there is debate over the “right” answer. This

 6           was an issue in the prior rate case, with Staff recommending an earnings band of 9.2% to

 7           10.5% against an ROE of 9.2%, and DOD/FEA recommending narrowing the dead band to

 8           +/- 0.5% around the authorized ROE of 9.1%. 75 Instead, the Commission found reasonable

 9           and approved the Company-proposed 9.5% to 12.0% ROE band. 76 This resulted in an ROE

10           dead band of -1% / +1.5% around the approved ROE, which was an expansion from the prior

11           -0.95% / +1.05% band that had been in place from 2014 to 2019. As discussed shortly, this

12           decision had material consequences for customers.

13   Q69.    HOW HAS THE COMPANY PERFORMED RELATIVE TO ITS AUTHORIZED ROE?

14   A69.    GPC has consistently over-performed relative to its authorized ROE, although the exact

15           amount was not produced because the Company dodged answering a discovery question on

16           this issue. When asked by Staff to “identify the incremental profit (in dollars) retained by

17           Georgia Power above the ROE set by the Commission for each year from 2013 through

18           2021”, the Company responded with a workpaper that identified earnings over the ROE dead

19           band approved by the Commission. 77 This subtle deflection obscures a critical data point that

20           Staff appeared to be seeking by asking for the incremental revenue and profit above “the

21           ROE” (singular, implying the central, approved ROE) set by the Commission, not the “REO

22           band” as answered by the Company.

23                   Despite its attempt to obscure the question asked, GPC’s response is revealing. In the

24           nine years between 2013 and 2022, the Company collected excess revenue in six years totally


     75
        2019 Rate Case Order at 5-6.
     76
        Id.

        STF-PIA-3-2 (emphasis added).

 1             $380 million above the top of the ROE dead band. 78 This response does not include excess

 2             revenue above the approved, central ROE value and below the top of the ROE band. Given

 3             the Company has not requested a special rate case in these years to address earnings below

 4             the bottom of the ROE threshold, it stands to reason that it has not under-earned below the

 6   Q70.      HOW DID THE COMPANY DISCUSS THESE RESULTS?

 7   A70.      In testimony, the Company touted the results as a “benefit” of the multi-year plan: “Not only

 8             have the three-year rate plans helped to keep rates stable and predictable, but the embedded

 9             sharing mechanism has also enabled the Company to provide approximately $297 million in

10             benefits back to customers since 2013.” 79

11                     This characterization is staggering in its bravado. To frame as a “benefit” to

12             customers the return of a portion of the nearly $400 million collected above and beyond a

13             generous ROE earnings dead band is Orwellian doublespeak. To accept this framing is to

14             accept the notion that the Company is entitled to unlimited upside profits from its operations

15             and that any limits on its earnings is a “benefit” for customers. This is simply not how a

16             monopoly’s earnings should work under the regulatory compact.

17   Q71.      HOW MUCH HAS THE COMPANY COLLECTED ABOVE ITS CENTRAL APPROVED ROE BUT

18             BELOW THE TOP OF THE ROE EARNINGS BAND?

19   A71.      [Mr. Lucas] This is an excellent question, and in fact was directly asked by Staff in

21             As I am unable to ask discovery, I was not able to ask a follow up question requesting this

22             information directly from the Company.

23                     That said, I was able to produce a reasonable estimate based on the Annual Retail

 1              its approved ROE but below the upper limit of the ROE dead band. This revenue is not

 5   Q72.       HOW DID YOU CALCULATE THIS FIGURE?

 6   A72.       [Mr. Lucas] I used the workpapers that were filed with each of the Company’s ASRs. 80

 7              These workpapers included a summary of the Company’s adjusted earnings and adjusted rate

 8              base; its cost of equity, debt, and capital structure; and a calculation of its actual ROE based

 9              on those values. Workpapers for years in which the Company reported excess earnings

10              above the ROE dead band had a “Sharing” tab that calculated the excess revenue and cost

11              share between customers and the Company.

12                      Using these files, I was able to simply update the ROE to the central approved value

13              and have the model calculate the excess revenue requirement collected. For years in which

14              the Company did not report an ROE above the top of the ROE band, I recreated the

15              “Sharing” tab to perform this calculation. I also reviewed the annual Staff filings and

16              Commission orders on the ASRs, some of which made adjustments to the ROE or reported

17              revenue collections found in the Company’s original filing.

 2   Q74.   WHAT ARE YOUR OBSERVATIONS ON THIS RESULT?

 3   A74.   [Mr. Lucas] I have several. First, despite the revenue sharing mechanism, the Company has

 4          been able to retain hundreds of millions of dollars per year of excess revenue above its

 5          central, approved ROE value before it has to return a single dollar to customers. While the

 6          ROE earnings dead band can be a useful policy construct when paired with multiyear rate

 7          plans, it is currently set too wide and too strongly favors the Company over customers. I

 8          urge the Commission to revisit the appropriateness of the current band, particularly the recent

 9          approval of the upside potential to 1.5% over ROE.

10                  Second, the Commission’s approved ROE is not functioning as a practical limit on

11          the Company’s earnings. If the Company had experienced years in which it earned less than

12          its ROE and years in which it earned more than its ROE, one could argue that its rates were

13          set correctly. But the persistent one-way prejudice towards overearning strongly suggests

14          that the Company rates are consistently set too high relative to the costs it actually incurs.

15          GPC has collected excess revenue in every single year since 2011, with an average of $135

16          million collected above the central ROE and below the top of the ROE dead band. The

17          smallest annual increment was an excess ROE of 0.41% worth $52 million; the largest was

18          2.23% worth $291 million. The Company’s actual ROE has averaged 12.15% over the past

19          12 years, 223 basis points higher than the average approved ROE of 10.92%.


                                                                                                               43

 1                    Third, the changes over time in GPC’s rate base and capital structure have

 2           exacerbated this issue. In 2011, the Company’s adjusted net rate base was $14.3 billion and

 3           its equity share set to 51.4%. 81 In 2021, its adjusted net rate base had grown 51% to $21.6

 4           billion and its equity share nearly 5% to 56.1%. 82 The combination of a larger rate base on

 5           which it earns a return, a higher share of equity in its capital stack, and the approval of a

 6           1.5% upside prior to any refunds to customers has resulted in huge increase in revenue

 7           collections above the ROE but below the upper ROE band. The Company had an ROE of

 8           12.14% in 2012 and collected $123 million above its ROE but below the upper ROE dead

 9           band. GPC’s 2021 ROE was lower at 12.09%, but it collected $244 million above its ROE

10           and below the upper ROE dead band.

14           was designed. However, I reviewed the total revenue collected per class from 2011 to 2021

15           using Energy Information Administration (“EIA”) data and applied the overcollection in

16           proportion to total revenue share. 83 These figures were divided by the number of residential

17           customers in each year to produce an average impact shown below in Table 5.

 6   Q76.   TURNING FULL CIRCLE, HOW DO THESE FIGURES COMPARE TO THE PURPORTED NEM COST

 7          SHIFT?

 8   A76.   They are also orders of magnitude larger. In 2021, the Company claims the total base rate

 9          revenue erosion from NEM is $1.4 million, or less than $0.20 per year for a customer using

10          1,000 kWh a month. In the past two years, the Company has collected more than 200 times

11          as much from residential customers from revenue above its approved ROE and below the

12          upper ROE band.

13   Q77.   PUTTING EVERYTHING TOGETHER, HOW DO THE VARIOUS REQUESTED AND PENDING COST

14          INCREASES AND EXCESS REVENUE COLLECTION COMPARE TO THE PURPORTED NEM COST

15          SHIFT?

16   A77.   There simply is no comparison. The NEM revenue erosion, even if taken at face value,

17          amounts to about $0.20 a year for a typical residential customer. Between the requests made

18          in this case, the projections of the currently-authorized Plant Vogtle costs, and the excess


                                                                                                           45

 1          revenue collected above the approved ROE but below the upper ROE dead band under the

 2          current structure, the annual bill for a typical residential customer will increase by about $34

 3          per month or more than $400 a year. This is more than 2,000 times as large of an impact and

 4          is the very definition of a red herring.

 6   Q78.   AS A PRACTICAL MATTER, GIVEN THE COMPANY HAS EXCEEDED ITS APPROVED ROE EVERY

 7          YEAR SINCE 2011, WHERE WOULD THE $1.4 MILLION IN REVENUE ADJUSTMENT GO?

 8   A78.   It would either go entirely to the Company (if its earnings are above the approved ROE and

 9          below the upper ROE band) or be refunded 80% to customers (if its earnings are above the

10          top of the ROE band). For example, if the revenue adjustment had been in place in 2020,

11          then the Company would have collected an additional $1.4 million in revenue below the top

12          of the ROE dead band. This would have been added to the $217 million that it already

13          collected and kept that year. If the adjustment had been in place in 2021, then 80% of the

14          adjustment would have been returned to customers since the Company’s ROE exceeded 12%.

15                  The only way that the $1.4 million in revenue erosion could represent a revenue

16          deficit as opposed to just more money collected by the Company is if GPC earned below its

17          approved ROE. And considering this is something that has never happened since the ASR

18          process was implemented, the overwhelming likelihood is all or part of the $1.4 million

19          adjustment would simply be added to the annual revenue overcollection.

20   Q79.   WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND WITH REGARD TO THIS ISSUE?

21   A79.   [Mr. Lucas] I strongly recommend the Commission revisit the ROE dead band that governs

22          the ARP. The decision to lower the central ROE to 10.5% in the last case while leaving the


                                                                                                          46

 1             top of the ROE band at 12% instead of lowering it to 11.5% resulted in GPC collecting and

 2             keeping $142 million more in 2020 and 2021 above the ROE but under the 12% upper dead

 3             band. 84 If the upper band had instead been set at 11.5%, $114 million (80% of the total)

 4             would have been returned to customers to help reduce current and future bills. Instead, GPC

 5             kept 100% of the excess.

 6                     Given the increase in rate base and equity share, I would recommend the Commission

 7             set the ROE band to +/- 50 basis points. The Company has clearly demonstrated that it is

 8             able to manage costs to maximize profitability, having never failed to exceed its approved

 9             ROE in the past 11 years, while exceeding a 12% ROE in 7 of the last 11. If the Commission

10             were to set the upper ROE band at 11% in 2021, the Company would have still kept $81

11             million before hitting the upper ROE band and having the shared savings mechanism kick in.

12             Combined with 20% of all revenue beyond 11%, there should be ample incentive for the

13             Company to continue to effectively maximize its profits while providing more protection to

14             customers against higher bills.


     84
          2020 and 2021 ASR Reports.


                                                                                                            47

 1    V.    GEORGIA POWER’S DEMAND-BASED “SMART USAGE” TARIFF IS FLAWED AND

 2          SHOULD NOT BE THE DEFAULT RATE FOR NEW PREMISES

 3   Q80.   WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF THIS SECTION OF YOUR TESTIMONY?

 4   A80.   In this section, we discuss the Company’s “Smart Usage” TOU-RD tariff. This is a three-

 5          part tariff consisting of a fixed customer charge, a peak/off peak energy charge, and a

 6          demand charge. We discuss the design of this tariff and how its use of a non-coincident peak

 7          (“NCP”) demand charge to collect a significant portion of a customer’s bill is fatally flawed.

 8          We also analyze the usage patterns of new customers added to this tariff since it became the

 9          default rate choice for new premises.

10   Q81.   PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR FINDINGS.

11   A81.   The TOU-RD tariff is not reflective of cost-causation and should not be the default

12          residential tariff. Its NCP billing demand is a faulty measure that does not consider the

13          timing of an individual customer’s peak demand. Further, it produces higher bills for most

14          customers on the tariff compared to the R, TOU-REO, or TOU-PEV options, particularly for

15          lower-than-average use customers. This exacerbates the problem of having the TOU-RD rate

16          as the default rate for new customers; those added to this tariff in the past two years are

17          disproportionally lower-than-average use customers and are experiencing significantly higher

18          bills than they would have on the R tariff.

19   Q82.   WHAT ARE YOUR RECOMMENDATIONS WITH REGARD TO THE TOU-RD RATE?

20   A82.   The Commission should require substantial modifications to the TOU-RD tariff. First, it

21          should no longer be the default tariff due to the all the reasons discussed below. Second, it

22          should be redesigned in one of two ways: with a TOU billing demand charge that recovers a

23          smaller portion of the fixed production, transmission, and distribution costs, or as an NCP

24          billing demand charge that only recovers low voltage distribution equipment close to the

25          customer. Third, the Commission should require the Company to update its billing

26          determinants to ensure that the rate is revenue neutral with respect to the R tariff. We discuss

27          each of these recommendations below.


                                                                                                            48

 1                    The Smart Usage Tariff is Improperly Designed and Should be Modified

 2   Q83.    PLEASE DESCRIBE THE TOU-RD TARIFF.

 3   A83.    The TOU-RD tariff is a three-part tariff, with a fixed customer charge, a volumetric energy

 4           charge based on total peak and off-peak kWh consumption, and an NCP demand charge

 5           based on the highest 60-minute billing demand in a month. Currently, customers pay about

 6           $14 per month for the customer charge, $0.096/kWh and $0.0103/kWh for peak and off-peak

 7           energy, respectively, and $8.21 per kW of peak demand for base rates. 85 Peak periods for

 8           energy consumption are between 2 and 7 PM weekdays from June to September, with all

 9           other hours (and summer holidays) being off-peak; there is no time constraints on the

10           maximum demand.

11   Q84.    IS THE CONCEPT OF “BILLING DEMAND” EASILY UNDERSTOOD OR EASILY CONTROLLED BY

12           TYPICAL CUSTOMERS?

13   A84.    No, we do not believe it is. While the Company’s literature suggests that customer can

14           simply stagger appliance use (such as the laundry, cooking dinner, or running the air

15           conditioner) at the same time to avoid high charges on the TOU-RD tariff, this concept is not

16           as easily done as it is made out to seem. 86

17                    Customers generally do not know how much power their appliances consume.

18           Laundry can have substantially different impacts on one’s energy use. Old dryers consume

19           substantially more energy than new ones, and gas dryers use much less electricity than

20           electric dryers. Further, the power demand depends on the temperature setting and duration

21           of the cycle. Likewise, cooking loads vary substantially based on equipment and preparation.

22           Modern induction cooktops can pull upwards of 10 kW of power, while oven power

23           consumption varies greatly depending on whether it is on a low bake or high broil setting.

24           Air condition is certainly a major contributor to power usage, but the types of air condition


     85
        TOU-RD-6 tariff. Customers separately pay for the various fuel, ECCR, and NCCR riders. Available at
     https://www.georgiapower.com/content/dam/georgia-power/pdfs/electric-service-tariff-pdfs/TOU-RD-6.pdf
     86
        https://www.georgiapower.com/residential/billing-and-rate-plans/pricing-and-rate-plans/smart-usage.html


                                                                                                                  49

 1             units can vary dramatically from small, window units in apartments to multiple 5+ ton units

 2             in detached single family homes.

 3                        On top of this, many appliances pull different amounts of power at different times in

 4             their usage cycle. Even if set to a constant temperature, air conditioners kick on and off

 5             depending on the ambient temperature, heating load from the sun, air circulation, insulation

 6             level, and many other factors. Similarly, running hot water in the sink for a few minutes may

 7             cause the electric water heater to kick in, pulling many kW of power without the customer’s

 8             knowledge or control. It is simply unrealistic to expect a customer to manually manage all

 9             the appliances in her house during every single hour of the month lest a single unfortunate

10             event conspire to set a customer’s peak demand for a month.

11                        Even worse, customers with electric vehicle chargers or electric space heaters with

12             high power draws that cannot be avoided indefinitely have no means of reducing their bills

13             under a certain level, even if they never used other appliances at the same time. This

14             effectively converts the demand-based tariff into an unavoidable fixed charge, which is

15             neither cost-reflective nor just and reasonable.

16   Q85.      DID THE COMPANY PROVIDE WORKPAPERS THAT DETAILED HOW THIS RATE WAS DESIGNED

17             OR WHAT COSTS THE ENERGY AND DEMAND CHARGES WERE DESIGNED TO RECOVER?

18   A85.      No, it did not. Staff asked in discovery “Please provide executable copies of all workpapers

19             and analyses utilized to develop the proposed rate schedule revenue increases and specific

20             rate designs for all aspects of this case.” 87 The workpapers that the Company provided did

21             not detail the composition of the various rate design components, but instead generally scaled

22             up each existing rate component (i.e., the base, energy, and demand rates) to collect the

23             targeted revenue increase. As such, there is no evidence in the record or Staff discovery on

24             how the rate is intended to recovery various costs from the COSS model.

 4   Q88.       WOULD YOU EXPECT THE MAPPING OF COSS EXPENSES TO TRANSLATE DIRECTLY TO THE

 5              RATE DESIGN?

 6   A88.       [Mr. Lucas] No, I would not. It is neither necessary nor appropriate to exactly map the COSS

 7              results into a rate design. Rate design should be reflective of the COSS results, but other

 8              considerations such as gradualism and customer understanding, acceptance, and actionability

 9              must also be factored in. For example, it is entirely appropriate to recover demand-based

10              costs through a volumetric energy charge (as all of the residential tariffs except TOU-RD do)

11              despite some of these costs being driven by customer demand.

12   Q89.       THAT SAID, HOW DO THE COST PROPORTIONS FROM THE COSS COMPARE TO THE TOU-RD

13              RATE REVENUE COLLECTION?

14   A89.       [Mr. Lucas] They are directionally similar. The Production – Variable cost category contains

15              fuel and variable O&M expenses that are a function of energy generated. The COSS places

16              about 30% of residential expenses into this category, while the rate collects about 40% and

17              XX% of revenue based on the energy usage of the Customer Database and GPC rate design

18              workpapers, respectively.

19                      The Production – Fixed, Transmission, and Distribution costs are typically allocated

20              based on various measures of peak demand (e.g., 4CP, 12CP, Class NCP). These functions


     90
          Exhibits LPE-1 and LPE-2


                                                                                                              52

 1           contain about 52% of the COSS expenses, while the rate collects about 48% and XX% of

 2           revenue through the demand charge for the Customer Database and GPC rate design

 3           workpapers, respectively.

 4                    Finally, the Customer costs are independent of energy or demand and pertain to the

5            costs of connecting customers to the system and back-office functions such as billing. The

6            COSS assigns about 10% of residential expenses to this category, while the rates collect

 7           about 12% and XX% of revenues through the Customer Database and GPC Rate Design

 8           workpapers, respectively.

 9   Q90.    WHAT DOES THIS MEAN?

10   A90.    [Mr. Lucas] It means that nearly all demand-based costs related to production, transmission,

11           and distribution are collected through the demand charge. While this may appear appropriate

12           given both are demand-based measures, a customer’s individual NCP demand level has

13           nothing to do with the coincident peak demand measures that drive costs in the COSS. This

14           is a fatal flaw in the rate design, and the Commission should direct GPC to redesign the rate

15           to correct this shortcoming.

16   Q91.    PLEASE EXPAND ON THIS POINT.

17   A91.    [Mr. Lucas] The core premise of the COSS is that customers are assigned costs in proportion

18           to their usage during hours that drive those costs. The system is sized to meet the peak

19           demands of its customer base; as such, costs are allocated based on each customer group’s

20           load during peak hours. In the Company’s COSS, production capacity costs are generally

21           allocated to customer classes based on a 12CP method, while transmission costs are generally

22           allocated based on a 4CP method. Distribution costs are allocated based on either the 4CP

23           (voltage levels C-E) or Class NCP (voltage levels F-G) demand levels. 91 Precisely zero costs

24           are allocated based on the independent peak demand of individual customers, even though


     91
       The Company allocates demand-based production costs and step-up substation costs on a 12CP basis, and high-
     voltage transmission costs on an 80% 4CP/ 20% 12CP basis. Lower-voltage transmission assets are allocated on a
     4CP or NCP basis depending on voltage level. Evans Direct at 17.


                                                                                                                  53

 1           there is a commonly used COSS allocator (sum of individual max demand) that captures this

 2           value.

 3                    A key consideration is rate design is that rates should be reflective of cost-causation;

 4           that is, if costs are driven by usage during peak hours, then the rate should reflect this and

 5           charge more for usage during peak hours. By designing the TOU-RD rate to capture most of

 6           the demand-based costs through an individual NCP demand charge, the Company is breaking

 7           the rule of cost-reflective rates.

 8                    From a COSS perspective, any customer who sets their individual peak usage during

 9           an hour that is not the Class NCP or one of the 12CP hours is necessarily not adding to the

10           peak demand on the system. Put another way, if the individual’s peak occurs in the any of

11           the 719 hours a month that are not the 12CP peak hour, or any of the 8,759 hours of the year

12           that are not the Class NCP hour, there is spare capacity on the system. 92 Only in the instance

13           that a customer sets their individual peak demand in the same hour as the Class NCP or one

14           of the 12CP hours could they potentially be adding marginal cost or changing the COSS

15           allocation values.

16                    But even in this case, the theoretical costs that a customer could impose are limited

17           by the physical reality of the system. If a customer’s individual maximum demand in April

18           coincides with the 12CP hour for April, even though the COSS allocates some costs based on

19           this hour, there is no incremental cost imposed on the physical system as the total load being

20           served in April is much lower than the total load served during the summer peaks. The stress

21           on all pieces of equipment during the highest load in April is still substantially lower than the

22           stress on all pieces of equipment during peak summer hours. If an asset can handle peak

23           loads in the summer, it necessarily can handle peak loads in April.

24   Q92.    WHAT DOES THIS MEAN FOR CUSTOMERS ON THE TOU-RD?


     92
       By definition, the peak hour is the one where the aggregate load of a class or tariff is highest, including the
     demand of the individual customer. All hours other than this hour necessarily have lower total demand, otherwise
     that hour would become the peak hour.


                                                                                                                     54

 1   A92.      The combination of these factors means that billing a customer for most of the demand-based

 2             production, transmission, and distribution costs based on their individual monthly peak

 3             demand does not follow the protocols of cost-reflective rates. The rate is unable to send price

 4             signals to reduce system costs because the measure of demand being used – the individual’s

5              peak demand – is not a driver of system costs.

6                        Further, the rate is not actionable. A customer is unaware of their billing demand

7              level until after they receive their bill, and even then, they are not provided with any useful

8              information about when or why they set their peak demand. The Company’s billing system

9              does not retain the time and date of when the customer’s peak demand is set, so not even this

10             basic information can be passed onto a customer’s bill. 93 With no knowledge of when they

11             set their peak or what occurred to make this happen, there is no ability for customers to

12             manage their peak demand.

13   Q93.      DO YOU HAVE AN EXAMPLE OF HOW THESE MISMATCHES CAN RESULT IN CUSTOMERS BEING

14             OVERCHARGED FOR THEIR USAGE?

15   A93.      A perfect example is a customer with an electric vehicle (“EV”) who always charges during

16             overnight hours, and whose EV charger is the largest power draw in their house. Residential

17             EV charges commonly draw 9.6 kW, and some more powerful units can pull 19.2 kW. A

18             customer who routinely charges overnight when there is spare capacity on the distribution,

19             transmission, and generation system is not driving any cost increases as the capacity of

20             system is capable of handling much more load.

21                       However, on the TOU-RD rate, this customer would lock in monthly charges of

22             nearly $80 for the 9.6 kW charger and over $150 for the 19.6 kW charger despite the fact that

23             these loads do not cause any demand-based costs on the system. Worse, because the

24             customer’s demand-based bill would be locked in every month from charger her car, there is

25             no incentive for the customer to manager her other loads (e.g., air conditioning, washing


     93
          STF-TAI-2-2.


                                                                                                                 55

 1          machine, etc.) during the actual high stress hours. This perverse incentive could cause her to

 2          increase her peak demand during hot summer afternoons which could contribute to higher

 3          costs while avoiding any cost responsibility for her usage.

 4   Q94.   WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND WITH REGARD TO THIS ISSUE?

 5   A94.   We are not proponents of demand charges for residential customers, particularly those

 6          offered without any supporting technology to help customers enable their demand. But given

 7          the TOU-RD rate is a voluntary rate and customers are not required to take service on it,

 8          there are a few ways the rate design could be improved.

 9                  The first would be redesigning the demand charge as a TOU demand charge. In this

10          structure, the demand rate would only be based on peak hours of the day, much like the

11          volumetric rate is higher during peak hours. These peak summer afternoons from 2 to 7

12          contain the hours in which the grid is under stress and where load reduction from customers

13          is most useful. Sending a price signal to manage demand during these hours is more

14          actionable than requiring customer to be hypervigilant over every hour of the month.

15                  The second would be to maintain the NCP demand charge but use it to only collect

16          low-voltage distribution costs near the customer’s premise (i.e., voltage levels G). While

17          these assets are still often shared among multiple customers, they have lower load

18          diversification than higher voltage assets. Using an individual’s NCP demand to collect this

19          small set of costs would be more reflective of cost-causation. In this structure, the

20          production, transmission, and higher-level distribution costs would be collected through

21          volumetric TOU rates.

22   Q95.   DO YOU HAVE THE DATA NEEDED TO DETERMINE WHAT THE RATES WOULD LOOK LIKE

23          UNDER YOUR PROPOSED STRUCTURE?

24   A95.   No, we do not. If the TOU demand charge was designed to collect most demand-based

25          production, transmission, and distribution costs during the four summer hours, it would likely

26          be set at a very high level (perhaps more than $20/kW). This may not be desirable as it could

27          lead to wide swings in bills based on an inadvertently high hour of usage and very high


                                                                                                         56

 1             summer bills. Instead, we recommend that a sizeable portion of the demand-based costs be

 2             recovered through a volumetric TOU rate to produce a demand charge in the $10-12 range.

 3             This would still send a robust price signal to reduce demand during summer peak hours but

 4             would prevent surges in summer bills.

 5                       If the NCP billing demand was kept but the collected costs reduced to low-voltage

 6             assets such as line transformers, based on the plant in service ratios from the COSS, we

 7             would expect the demand rate to fall to around $1/kW. While this is much lower than the

 8             demand charge in the current TOU-RD design, it is all that should reasonably be collected

 9             through an NCP-based billing demand given the inherent lack of cost-reflection in this

10             metric.


11                   Low Use Customers are Substantially Overcharged on the Smart Use Tariff

12   Q96.      DID YOU ANALYZE HOW CUSTOMERS WITH DIFFERENT USAGE LEVELS FARED ON THE

13             VARIOUS RESIDENTIAL RATES?

14   A96.      [Mr. Lucas] Yes, I did. Staff asked for monthly usage data from each residential customer in

15             2021. 94 Using this data set, I was able to perform several analyses on how customers taking

16             service on the TOU-RD tariff would have fared on the TOU-PEV, TOU-REO, and R rates. I

17             began by trimming the data to only include customers with a full 12 months of recorded data.

18             Given the TOU-RD rate experienced substantial customer growth during 2021 due to the

19             decision to make it the default rate at the beginning of this year, this reduced the data set

20             from about 61,000 customers with partial months of data to about 11,000 customers with 12

21             months of data. While this does represent a sizable reduction to the data set, these are also

22             customers who self-selected into this tariff prior to it become the default. In other words,

23             they affirmatively chose to be on this tariff.

24                       After trimming the data, I recreated each TOU-RD customer’s bill under the other

25             tariffs, including fuel cost and the various adder tariffs. Because the data set did not


     94
          STF-TAI-2-2.


                                                                                                               57

 1             specifically break down peak, off-peak, and super off-peak usage for these customers, and I

 2             was unable to ask for the data myself, I calculated the tariff average peak, off-peak, and super

 3             off-peak values from another data set the Company provided to Staff. 95 Given that an

 4             individual’s bill may contain relatively more or less peak or super off-peak energy than the

 5             tariff average, I also ran sensitivities around these figures.

 6   Q97.      WHAT DID YOUR ANALYSIS SHOW?

 7   A97.      [Mr. Lucas] In theory, since this is a voluntary rate, and all customers who were taking

 8             service under it as of January 2021 self-selected into the rate, one might have expected the

 9             group of customers to have lower bills compared to other tariffs. However, as is clearly

10             evident, this is not the case. My analysis showed that even for these self-selecting customers,

11             most experienced higher annual bills on the TOU-RD tariff than they would have had on the

12             R (61%), TOU-REO (63%), and TOU-PEV (72%) tariff. Moreover, for customers below the

13             median usage, nearly all customers experienced higher bills on the TOU-RD rate than the R

14             (90%), TOU-REO (89%), or TOU-PEV (94%) tariffs.

15                        Figure 1 is a summary of the results comparing the TOU-RD tariff to the R tariff. In

16             this chart, the blue dots represent the bill increase (above the 0% line) or bill decrease (below

17             the 0% line) shown on the left y axis against the average monthly usage on the x axis. The

18             orange line shows on the right y axis the cumulative percentage of customers up to that usage

19             level that have higher bills on the TOU-RD rate than the R rate shown. The vertical purple

20             line represents the median usage of this customer set, equal to 971 kWh per month.

 3                                          The chart shows a clear relationship between bills on these two tariffs. Small

 5                               tariff. For customers that average less than 500 kWh per month, 97% pay more on the TOU-

 6                               RD rate than they would have on the R rate. By the time the median usage of 971 kWh is

14                               about half of the customers experience lower bills. By the time usage reaches 2,000 kWh a

15                               month (roughly double the median usage) do customers consistently save on the TOU-RD

16                               tariff. All told, 61% of all customers in the data set – regardless of usage – had higher bills
