10   Q100. DO THESE RESULTS SURPRISE YOU?

11   A100. [Mr. Lucas] While the magnitude of the impact on low-use customers is surprising, it is not

12            surprising that low-use customers fare poorly on this tariff. Customers with low monthly

13            usage tend to have lower load factors. 96 And customers with low load factors are penalized

14            by rate designs with NCP demand charges that collect a substantial portion of the overall

15            tariff revenue. I calculated the load factor in the data set, and unsurprisingly, low-use

16            customers had low load factors as seen in Figure 4 below.


     96
       A load factor is a fraction that represents the total usage in a period divided by the maximum demand in the period
     times the number of hours. A load factor of 1.0 represents constant usage at a set level, while a low value such as
     0.1 represents occasional high peak demands but low overall usage.


                                                                                                                       61

                                                            Average Monthly Load Factor vs. Monthly Usage
                                            0.80

                                            0.70


              Average Monthly Load Factor
                                            0.60

                                            0.50

                                            0.40

                                            0.30

                                            0.20

                                            0.10

                                              -
                                                   -          500         1,000      1,500      2,000     2,500      3,000   3,500   4,000
                                                                                      Average Monthly Usage
 1
 2                                                                  Figure 4 - TOU-RD Customer Average Monthly Load Factor


 3                                                     Even worse, customers with low NCP load factors have low coincident factors. This

 4                                means their individual peak demand is not aligned with system peak hours when high loads

 5                                actually drive costs. This relationship, dubbed the Bary Curve, has been known for decades

 6                                and was discussed by the Edison Electric Institute in its 1984 publication “The Art of Rate

 7                                Design.” 97 In summary, high NCP demand from low-use customers is not putting stress on

 8                                the system because the demand spikes tend to fall outside peak hours.

 9   Q101. WERE YOU ABLE TO ANALYZE THE GPC CUSTOMER SET TO EXPLICITLY DEMONSTRATE

10                                THIS?

11   A101. [Mr. Lucas] No, I was not because I was not able to ask for hourly data for a sample of

12                                customers. However, in another case in which I was able to ask discovery, I did obtain this

13                                information and performed this analysis. DTE Energy in Michigan produced a workpaper

14                                that showed the three highest load hours in each month in 2018 and 2020 for 10,000 random

15                                residential customers. I analyzed the data to determine how many of those hours

16                                corresponding with the 4CP, 12CP, and Class NCP hours.

     97
          The Art of Rate Design, Chapter 5, Frank S. Walters, Edison Electric Institute, 1984.


                                                                                                                                             62

 1                 On average, only 1% of the 10,000 customers had their individual peak demand in a

 2          month fall during one of the 4CP hours. Even fewer experienced alignment with the 12CP

 3          hours. The Class NCP metric was the “highest” of the three, with a mere 1.33% of customers

 4          experiencing their monthly peak at the same time as the class peak.
                                        Individual Customer Monthly Highest Load
                    COSS Hour           1st Highest     2nd Highest    3rd Highest
                    4CP                    1.03%          0.91%           0.81%
                    12CP                   0.78%          0.59%           0.51%
                    Class NCP              1.33%          1.08%           1.02%
 5                          Table 10 - DTE Energy Individual Peak Hour Coincidence Analysis


 6                 Further, these customers were not limited to low-use, low-load factor customers,

 7          whose overlap between individual peak demand and system peak demand was even lower. I

 8          performed the same analysis on customers with rooftop PV who, due to self-consumption,

 9          had lower load factors than a non-PV customer. There were only 4 instances over the 1,348

10          4CP customer-hours – 0.3% – when the customer’s highest load hour of the month

11          corresponded to the 4CP hours. Similarly, only 0.5% of peak hours occurred during the

12          12CP hours. And there was only one single instance where a DPV customer’s peak hour fell

13          during the Class NCP peak. These results reinforce the futility of attempting to send a price

14          signal with an NCP billing demand structure; individual customers simply do not peak during

15          the same hours that drive system costs.

16   Q102. PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR ANALYSIS ON THE TOU-RD TARIFF THUS FAR.

17   A102. [Mr. Lucas] I began by analyzing data from a set of 11,000 TOU-RD customers who had

18          been on the tariff for all twelve months of 2021. I found that nearly all low-use customers on

19          this tariff paid substantially more than they would have on the R, TOU-REO, or TOU-PEV

20          rates. I showed that the low-use customers also had low load factors, which strongly

21          contributed to their higher bills on an NCP tariff. I also showed that residential customers

22          from another utility had extremely low levels of correlation between individual peak demand

23          hours and system peak demand hours.


                                                                                                           63

 1                       This analysis shows that the TOU-RD rate is poorly designed and is actually harming

 2              many of customers that self-selected into the tariff. It also provides quantitative support for

 3              my assertion that the NCP billing demand structure does not send meaningful price signals. I

 4              now turn to implications of making this rate the default rate for residential customers.


 5   New Customers are Disproportionately Low Use Customers and are Being Harmed by the Default Rate

 6   Q103. WHAT IS A DEFAULT RATE?

 7   A103. A default rate is the rate that a new customer is placed on at the commencement of their

 8              electric service. Customers have the option to choose other rates for which they qualify, but

 9              absent an affirmative choice, they are placed on the default rate.

10   Q104. WHAT IS THE CURRENT DEFAULT RATE FOR RESIDENTIAL CUSTOMERS?

11   A104. The TOU-RD rate was set as the default rate beginning in January 2021 as part of the 2019

12              Rate Case settlement agreement. 98 The Company was required to “report back to the

13              Commission at the time of its next base rate case regarding the adoption of TOU-RD and its

14              use as the default rate for newly constructed residential premises.”

15   Q105. WHAT DID THE COMPANY REPORT IN ITS FILING REGARDING THE ADOPTION OF THE TOU-

16              RD AS THE DEFAULT RATE?

17   A105. There was only one reference to the word “TOU-RD” in the Company’s entire application,

18              and it was in response to its proposal to close the R tariff to new customers:
19                       Since the beginning of 2020, Georgia Power has proactively promoted its wide
20                       variety of residential rate options to help and encourage customers to choose the rate
21                       that best meets their needs when establishing service. As a result, customer adoption
22                       of alternative residential rate options such as Smart Usage (“TOU-RD”), Plug-in
23                       Electric Vehicle (“TOU-PEV”), Pay by Day (“PBD”) and FlatBill has increased
24                       significantly. Indeed, the number of residential customers who have chosen to
25                       participate in a time-of-use rate has increased by more than 250%. Many customers
26                       have demonstrated both a demand and preference for these rate plans. By closing the
27                       R tariff to new premises, the Company will continue to transition new residential
28                       accounts onto other rates within the Domestic Group that better align with the cost to
29                       serve customers. Again, the R tariff will remain available to existing residential
30                       premises on Georgia Power’s system that established service prior to January 2023.

          2019 Rate Case Order at 14.

 5             discovery questions to extract any additional details from the Company.

 6   Q107. WHAT DID THOSE DISCOVERY RESPONSE SHOW?

 7   A107. It showed that customers who have been placed on the TOU-RD rate since January 2021

 8             have experienced higher bills than they would have on the R tariff. Further, many customers

 9             that were placed on the tariff in 2021 left after a few months. And in contrast with the

10             Company’s statement above about the increases in the number of customers on the various

11             TOU rates, the only rate that has been appreciable growth is the TOU-RD rate after becoming

12             the default rate.

19             tariff at some point in 2021. These do not include customers who disconnected their service

20             during the year, as indicated in the data set as a customer whose last bill was prior to

21             December.

 3   Q113. DID GPC’S DATA SUPPORT YOUR EARLIER ANALYSIS ABOUT LOWER USE CUSTOMERS

 4          GETTING HIGHER BILLS ON THE TOU-RD TARIFF?

 5   A113. [Mr. Lucas] Yes. The Company provided the average bill that new TOU-RD customers

 6          received along with what they would have received on the R tariff. As with my analysis, the

 7          Company’s data shows a substantial premium on the TOU-RD tariff. Over the span of 17

 8          months of data provided by the Company, customers were charged an average total of $1,250

 9          on the TOU-RD tariff, while they would have only been charged $984 on the R tariff. The

10          cost of the “default” choice was a 27% premium on their bills.

 3   Q114. DID CUSTOMERS THAT WERE SIGNED UP BY DEFAULT ON THE TOU-RD TARIFF RECEIVE

 4                     INFORMATION ABOUT OTHER RATES THAT WERE AVAILABLE TO THEM?

 5   A114. Not directly. When asked by Staff what information customers newly added to the TOU-RD

 6                     rate receive, 104 the Company responded, “New customers to the Smart Usage rate (TOU-RD)

 7                     receive a welcome letter that confirms their enrollment and provides helpful information to

 8                     educate them on how to take advantage of the rate’s design.” It included a copy of a letter

 9                     that is mailed to the new customer. 105 Nowhere in this letter is the customer provided with

10                     information about other rate options, including those that may save the customer more money

11                     than the TOU-RD rate.

12   Q115. WHAT IS YOUR CONCLUSION BASED ON THIS AND YOUR PRIOR ANALYSES?

13   A115. It is entirely inappropriate for the TOU-RD rate as currently designed to be the default rate.

14                     The rate is not cost-aligned given the use of individual customer NCP billing demand to

15                     collect shared costs for production, transmission, and distribution. It causes higher bills for


     104
           STF-PIA-2-3.
     105
           STF-PIA-2-3 Attachment A.


                                                                                                                         71

 1          more than 90% of customers that use less than the median amount of energy compared to

 2          alternative rates. It appears not to be revenue neutral with respect to the other tariffs.

 3                  The TOU-RD rate is the only rate that has seen substantial growth in the past two

 4          years, largely due to the decision to make it the default rate in January 2021. But the new

 5          customers being added to the tariff are disproportionately low use customers. As a result,

 6          they are being penalized through bills that are 27% higher on average than if they had been

 7          on the R tariff.

 8                  These facts are presumably all known to the Company. It knows that new customers

 9          are paying more on the TOU-RD rate than they would on the R rate because it produced data

10          that confirmed this fact. And despite a direct requirement from the Commission in the prior

11          rate case to report on these matters, the Company was conspicuously silent on the issue. The

12          Company now requests to close the R tariff and the TOU-REO tariffs to new premises, which

13          would close all but one conventional post-pay option (TOU-PEV) for new customers. At the

14          same time, it actively sows confusion about the TOU-PEV rate to muddy the waters on

15          eligibility. The net result of these actions is to make customers pay more for their electricity

16          service despite them not causing a corresponding increase in cost.

17   Q116. WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND WITH RESPECT TO THE TOU-RD TARIFF?

18   A116. The Commission should require substantial modifications to the TOU-RD tariff. First, it

19          should no longer be the default tariff due to the all the reasons discussed above. Second, it

20          should be redesigned in one of two ways: with a TOU billing demand charge that recovers a

21          smaller portion of the fixed production, transmission, and distribution costs, or as an NCP

22          billing demand charge that only recovers low voltage distribution equipment close to the

23          customer. Third, the Commission should require the Company to update its billing

24          determinants to ensure that the rate is revenue neutral with respect to the R tariff.


                                                                                                            72

 1   VI.    THE COMMISSION SHOULD STRIVE TO INCREASE FAIRNESS AND

 2          TRANSPARENCY IN RATE CASES AND OPTIONALITY IN RATE DESIGN

 3   Q117. WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF THIS SECTION OF YOUR TESTIMONY?

 4   A117. In this section, we discuss various components of the Company’s filing regarding availability

 5          of certain tariffs, technology tools that can aid or hinder customer education, and technical

 6          issues that impact the Company’s COSS. While these changes are not as individually

 7          impactful as those discussed above, they have an overall effect of reducing transparency and

 8          fairness and customer optionality.

 9   Q118. PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR FINDINGS.

10   A118. The Company’s proposal to close the R and TOU-REO customers to new customers should

11          be denied. There is little policy support for this step, and the Company produced no evidence

12          supporting its claim that customers would be “confused” if the TOU-REO remained active.

13          Worse, the Company is actively sowing confusion among its customers with its public facing

14          materials on the TOU-PEV rate and its “Solar Advisor Tool”. Finally, the Company’s

15          position on two technical issues related to the COSS are confusing at best, particularly related

16          to the supposed inaccuracy of its AMI data.

17   Q119. WHAT ARE YOUR RECOMMENDATIONS FROM THIS SECTION?

18   A119. The Commission should require the R and TOU-REO tariffs to remain open to all customers

19          and set the TOU-REO tariff as the default rate for new residential premises. It should direct

20          GPC to rename and rebrand its TOU-PEV tariff to undo the blatantly misleading information

21          regarding the purpose and eligibility requirements of this tariff. The Solar Advisor Tool

22          should be scrapped as it is beyond salvage. Finally, the Commission should direct the

23          Company to use the 4CP allocator for production costs and use actual AMI data instead of

24          statistical load research data for its COSS and rate design workpapers.


25                The Residential and Nights and Weekends Tariffs Should Remain Available

26   Q120. WHAT IS THE COMPANY’S PROPOSAL REGARDING THE R AND TOU-REO TARIFFS?


                                                                                                            73

 1   A120. The Company proposes to close both tariffs to new premises as of January 1, 2023. 106

 2   Q121. WHAT IS THE JUSTIFICATION GIVEN FOR CLOSING THE R TARIFF TO NEW PREMISES?

 3   A121. The Company claims that its proposal “is the logical next step in the Company’s ongoing

 4            efforts to encourage residential customer to move towards more modern rate structures…

 5            Many customers have demonstrated both a demand and preference for these rate plans. By

 6            closing the R tariff to new premises, the Company will continue to transition new residential

 7            accounts onto other rates within the Domestic Group that better align with the cost to serve

 8            customers.” 107

 9   Q122. DID THE COMPANY PERFORM ANY ANALYSIS ON OTHER UTILITIES THAT ARE PROPOSING TO

10            ELIMINATE OR REDUCING STANDARD VOLUMETRIC RATE OPTIONS FOR RESIDENTIAL

11            CUSTOMERS?

12   A122. No. When asked this question, the Company responded, “The Company has not performed a

13            specific analysis of which utilities or jurisdictions are eliminating standard volumetric rate

14            options for residential customers.” 108 The Company notes that utilities are increasingly

15            exploring additional rate options that are more reflective of cost of service, but offered no

16            evidence of utilities actively eliminating rates.

17   Q123. DID THE COMPANY PROVIDE ANY SUPPORT FOR ITS CLAIM THAT “MANY CUSTOMERS HAVE

18            DEMONSTRATED BOTH A DEMAND AND PREFERENCE FOR THESE RATE PLANS.”

19   A123. In what should be clearly noticed as a trend in this docket, the Company provided no details

20            in its filing about these preferences. Instead, Staff was required to ask two rounds of

21            discovery to get any documentation to support this statement. In the end, the Company

22            provided a report summarizing its 2022 survey on residential rates. All of the results of the

23            report were redacted as trade secret, despite the fact that there is no competitor to GPC’s

24            monopoly control over residential customers.

25   Q124. WHAT WAS YOUR IMPRESSION OF THE SURVEY?

     106
         Legg Direct at 15.
     107
         Legg Direct at 8.
     108
         STF-TIA-1-29.


                                                                                                               74

 1   A124. [Mr. Lucas] We have no ability to dispute what individual customers think of the service that

 2          GPC is providing to them. That said, surveys are carefully constructed, between the choice

 3          of who to call, when to call, and how to phrase the questions. The survey was crafted with

 4          very carefully worded questions such as “xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx

 5          xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx” and “xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx

 6          xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx” rather than the more direct “I understand how to use electricity

 7          on the rate to save money.” These subtle differences are intentional. The only direct

 8          comments about saving money or not on a given rate came from free-form comments to the

 9          generic “xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx xxxxxxxx” However, absent an actual rate

10          comparison tool (which is discussed in more detail below), customers are not able to tell if

11          they are on the best rate for their usage patterns even if they believe they are saving money

12          on the rate.

13                    Given this, it is telling that many of the free-form comments that the report

14          referenced on the TOU-RD were xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx

 5   Q125. WHAT IS THE JUSTIFICATION FOR THE CHANGE TO THE TOU-REO TARIFF?

 6   A125. The Company claims that closing this rate, which has been around since 1994, to customers

 7           at new premises “will avoid the confusion that could arise from having three competing time

 8           of use tariff options.” 111

 9   Q126. DOES THE COMPANY PROVIDE ANY SUPPORT WHATSOEVER THAT THE “CONFUSION” IT

10           SEEKS TO AVOID EXISTS?

11   A126. No, it does not. There is no supporting documents or studies that demonstrate any confusion

12           exists. Further, given this rate has been available to customers for nearly 30 years, it is

13           difficult to see how confusion would suddenly emerge now.

14   Q127. ARE THERE DIFFERENCES BETWEEN THE TOU-REO AND TOU-PEV TARIFFS THAT MERIT

15           KEEPING BOTH RATES AVAILABLE?

16   A127. Yes. These two tariffs are similar in that they both contain a volumetric TOU rate

17           component, but the off-peak rates are quite different. The TOU-REO tariff contains two

18           TOU periods, while the TOU-PEV adds a super off-peak. The details of the rates are in

19           Table 13 below, showing the current base rate plus fuel rider.
                             Characteristic       TOU-REO        TOU-PEV
                             Peak Hours           2-7 PM Weekdays June – Sept
                             Peak Rate                     $0.240219
                             Super Off Peak Hours     N/A      11 PM to 7 AM
                             Super Off-Peak Rate      N/A        $0.039512
                             Off Peak Rate         $0.079467     $0.099043
20                                   Table 13 - TOU-REO and TOU-PEV Rate Comparison


     110
         xx% of total respondents indicated they xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx said they xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx, while
     xx% said they xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx
     111
         Id.


                                                                                                             76

 1                        Both rates share the peak TOU periods and rate. The TOU-PEV adds a low-cost

 2              overnight component, which necessarily pushes up the off-peak component. In fact, the off-

3               peak rate on the TOU-PEV tariff is 25% higher than the off-peak rate on the TOU-REO

4               tariff. For customers with higher overnight usage, the TOU-PEV rate might be a good

5               choice. But for customers who do not use much energy overnight, the TOU-PEV rate would

6               be more expensive given the increase in the off-peak rate when most of their consumption

7               occurs.

 8   Q128. DID THE COMPANY PERFORM AN ANALYSIS THAT SUPPORTS THIS CLAIM?

 9   A128. Yes. In 2020, the Company performed a rate impact analysis for customers on the TOU-

10              REO and TOU-PEV tariffs. 112 It found that fully 76% of customers on both rates would pay

11              more on the TOU-PEV rate than they would on the TOU-REO rate. As seen below in Figure

12              9, most of these customers would see bill increases on the TOU-REO tariff of between 2%

13              and 4% ($10-$100 in this data set), with about 20% (those with sufficient overnight usage)

14              saving more than 1% on the TOU-PEV rate.

                                                                                                             77

 1   Q129. IF THE COMPANY’S PROPOSALS ARE APPROVED, WHAT WOULD HAPPEN TO CUSTOMERS

 2           FROM NEW PREMISES SIGNING UP FOR SERVICE?

 3   A129. New customers are not eligible for the Flat Bill tariff (which is effectively the R tariff with a

 4           risk premium adder) as they do not have a year of historical usage on which to base the

 5           charge. Thus, they would only have two traditional post-paid options: TOU-RD and TOU-

 6           PEV. The TOU-RD rate has serious deficiencies as discussed earlier in my testimony, while

 7           the TOU-PEV would result in a bill increases over the R tariff for low-use customers and

 8           over the TOU-REO for most new customers.

 9   Q130. WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND WITH RESPECT TO THESE RATES?

10   A130. We respectfully recommend the Commission require GPC to continue offering both tariffs

11           for new residential premises. While the R tariff does not have a time of use component, the

12           summer inclining block structure appropriately charges low-use customers less based on their

13           lower contribution to summer system peak demands that drives production and transmission

14           costs. 113 Although there is merit in moving more new customers to TOU-based rates, the

15           Company has not provided justification for closing the R tariff or evidence of other utilities

16           taking the same extreme step.

17                    Further, if the TOU-REO tariff was also closed, customers would either need to move

18           onto the TOU-RD rate (which as analyzed earlier charges a substantial premium to low-use

19           customers) or to the TOU-PEV rate (with its much higher off-peak rate that causes bill

20           increases for most customers). The TOU-REO rate continues to serve a purpose for

21           customers without substantial enough overnight usage to save money. It maintains a lower

22           off-peak rate more consistent with the Block 1 rate from the R tariff while still sending a

23           price signal to reduce usage during peak hours that drive system costs.


     113
        As discussed in Section V, low use customers tend to have low load factors, and customers with low load factors
     tend to have low coincidence factors.

 1            Georgia Power is Misleading Customers about the Plug-In EV Rate and Should be Stopped

 2   Q131. DOES THE PLUG-IN EV RATE REQUIRE A CUSTOMER TO OWN AN EV TO QUALIFY FOR THE

 3              RATE?

 4   A131. No, it does not.

 5   Q132. WOULD THE AVERAGE CUSTOMER BE ABLE TO DETERMINE THIS FROM THE COMPANY’S

 6              WEBPAGE?

 7   A132. [Mr. Lucas] No, I do not believe they would. The Company’s public-facing content

 8              regarding the Plug-In EV rate (discussed here as the TOU-PEV rate) consistently and

 9              strongly implies that EV ownership is a necessary prerequisite for qualifying for this tariff.

10              In fact, when I first reviewed the Company’s website content on this rate, I was surprised to

11              learn that an EV was not required.

12   Q133. DO YOU HAVE ANY EVIDENCE TO SUPPORT THE IDEA THAT MANY IF NOT MOST CUSTOMERS

13              ON THE EV RATE DO IN FACT OWN EVS?

14   A133. [Mr. Lucas] Yes. I analyzed hourly data from the TOU-PEV, TOU-REO, and R tariffs

15              provided by the Company. 114 The load profile between 7 AM and 11 PM is very similar

16              across the tariffs, with both of the TOU rates showing a drop in peak usage between 2 PM

17              and 7 PM compared to the R tariff. I created a “Scaled TOU-PEV” load profile by taking the

18              average ratio of the TOU-PEV and R usage in off-peak and peak hours and applying it to the

19              R usage during the super off-peak hours. There was a dramatic increase in usage on the

20              TOU-PEV rate starting at 11 PM that gradually narrowed as the super off-peak period

21              concluded at 7 AM, as evidenced by the difference between the actual and scaled TOU-PEV

22              usage (solid and dashed blue line, respectively, in Figure 10 below).

 3   Q134. WHAT DOES THIS CHART SHOW?

 4   A134. [Mr. Lucas] It shows that customers on the TOU-PEV tariff consistently – across all months,

 5                    seasons, and days of the week – add an average of about 1.5 kW of demand at the strike of 11

 6                    PM when the super off-peak rate starts. This increase falls over the next few hours until it

 7                    largely vanishes by 7 PM. Programmable thermostats cannot explain the data as the leap

 8                    occurred in winter (electric heating but no cooling or gas heating loads), summer (cooling but

 9                    no heating loads), and shoulder months (neither much heating nor cooling load).

10                                          There are few loads that can consistently add that much demand at the same time or

11                    that would show the gradual decay over the super off-peak period as charging an EV. As

12                    mentioned, home chargers commonly pull 8-10 kW of power. Not every EV user charges

13                    every night, so the fact the step increase is an average of 1.5 kW instead of 8 kW is not

14                    surprising. Further, not every EV is charged from a fully depleted state, meaning that all

15                    charged cars will charge at the beginning of the super off-peak period, some will charge for

16                    half of the super off-peak period, and a few will charge for whole super off-peak period.

17                    This perfectly explains the decay shown above.

25             Electric Vehicle” rate. The Company could name it something description like “Overnight

26             Savings” or “Super Off-Peak Savings.” The rate provides a reduced rate to all overnight

27             usage, not just for customers charging an EV.

 1                  Time and time again on GPC’s website, the Company conflates eligibility of the

 2          TOU-PEV tariff with driving an EV: “If you drive an electric vehicle”, “Electric vehicle

3           owner?”, “Drive an electric vehicle?”, “$19 to month to charge on PEV”. These are not

4           isolated incidents; all these mentions of the rate on its webpage includes a discussion of EVs.

 5   Q138. DO YOU BELIEVE THE TOU-PEV RATE IS A WELL-DESIGN AND USEFUL RATE?

 6   A138. [Mr. Lucas] Yes, I do. While I was not able to scrutinize the source data behind the rate

 7          design, the TOU-PEV tariff appears to be well-designed. It has a relatively high on-peak

 8          energy rate that is constrained to summer weekdays when the system is under stress and

 9          sends a robust price signal for customers to reduce or shift usage from this period. It offers a

10          very low overnight super off-peak rate that appears to be intended to only collect fuel and

11          variable O&M costs and not to collect capacity costs which are not driven by usage during

12          this time period.

13                  These are hallmarks of good rate design, but as with any single rate design, the rate is

14          not a perfect match for all customers. Lower use customers with normal overnight usage will

15          not fare well on this tariff. The TOU-PEV off-peak rate – where most of a lower-use

16          customer’s consumption will fall – is 13% higher than the Block 1 rate on the R tariff and

17          25% higher than the TOU-REO off-peak rate. For these customers, other rate options will

18          provide better experiences. On the other hand, customers who use electric space heating or

19          heat pumps may benefit from the lower overnight costs on this tariff compared to the R or

20          TOU-REO rate.

21   Q139. IF THIS RATE IS WELL-DESIGNED AND COULD HELP NON-EV OWNERS, WHY WOULD THE

22          COMPANY ALLOW CUSTOMER TO BE CONFUSED ABOUT THEIR ELIGIBILITY ON THIS RATE?

23   A139. [Mr. Lucas] It is a good question, and one that I would have liked to ask additional discovery

24          about. Absent that, I can only turn to the numbers. And the numbers confirm that, quite

25          simply, GPC makes more money if customers are on the TOU-RD tariff than if they were on

26          the TOU-PEV tariff. In the Customer Database data set discussed above in Section V, 72%

27          of customers with 12 months of usage experienced higher bills on the TOU-PEV tariff than


                                                                                                          84

 1          on the TOU-TOU-RD tariff, with an average increase in annual bills of 23%. In total, just for

 2          this set of 11,000 customers, the Company collected nearly $1 million or 5% more revenue

 3          than it would have if they were on the TOU-PEV rate.

 4                  If one ramps this up to all new premises, GPC’s additional revenue would scale

 5          proportionately. In fact, when I reanalyzed the complete Customer Database for all

 6          customers on the TOU-RD rate, the figures were even worse. With the complete sample of

 7          61,000 customers with at least one month of service on the TOU-RD rate, 90% of all

 8          customers had higher bills in 2021 on the TOU-RD rate than they would have had on the

 9          TOU-PEV rate. The Company earned an extra $6.8 million on these customers in 2021, a

10          staggering 19.7% increase over what they would have collected if these customers were on

11          the TOU-PEV tariff.

12                  The large difference between the 12-month customers and the full data set is due to

13          the addition of lower-use customers in 2021 onto a tariff which is particularly poorly suited

14          for them. I would expect if this analysis were repeated next year with more customers with

15          12 months of usage that the revenue collection difference between the TOU-RD and TOU-

16          PEV rate would increase substantially from the 5% mentioned above.

17   Q140. HOW DO THESE FIGURES COMPLEMENT YOUR ANALYSES ON THE INTENTIONAL CONFUSION

18          OF THE TOU-PEV RATE?

19   A140. The throughline is evident when looked at holistically. First, the Company petitioned for and

20          received permission to make the TOU-RD rate the default for new residential premises.

21          Then, the Company proposes to close the TOU-REO and R tariffs, which would force new

22          premises onto the TOU-RD or TOU-PEV rate. At the same time, it obscures the eligibility of

23          customers without an EV to take service on the TOU-PEV. This increases the number of

24          new customers who stay on the TOU-RD tariff – most of whom pay more – and decreases

25          new premise additions and existing customer switching to the TOU-PEV rate. The end result

26          is GPC makes more money.

27   Q141. WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND ON THIS ISSUE?


                                                                                                            85

 1   A141. The Commission should require GPC to rename the TOU-PEV rate, modify its webpage

 2          materials, and update its customer service protocols to clarify that all customers can sign up

 3          for the rate independent of their ownership or operation of an EV. It should also highlight the

 4          potential benefits of customers who use electric space heating, who may receive lower bills

 5          relative to other rates from overnight heating use.


 6                  The Default Rate Should be Changed to the Nights and Weekends Tariff

 7   Q142. BASED ON THE TOTALITY OF YOUR TESTIMONY IN THIS CASE, WHAT DO YOU BELIEVE IS THE

 8          CORRECTION DEFAULT OPTION FOR RESIDENTIAL CUSTOMERS?

 9   A142. We believe the “Nights and Weekends” TOU-REO rate should be set as the default tariff.

10          Customers should still have the option to opt out of this rate onto any other available rate,

11          including the R rate. The Company has failed to present information required by the

12          Commission in the 2019 Rate Case order to report on the TOU-RD tariff as the default rate.

13          While not a substitute for the Company’s obligation, we believe our testimony has provided

14          some useful details. In summary,
15              •   The TOU-RD rate is poorly designed and not reflective of cost-causation principles
16              •   The TOU-RD rate substantially overcharges low-use customers relative to the costs
17                  they incur on the system
18              •   New remises added to the TOU-RD rate in 2021 experienced much higher bills than
19                  they would have on the R, TOU-REO, or TOU-PEV rate

20                  These are simply not good characteristics for a default rate. By contrast, the TOU-

21          REO rate has been around for nearly 30 years, is simple to understand, and can provide bill

22          savings for customers who are able to shift usage out of the peak 2 PM to 7 PM summer

23          weekday window while reducing systems costs incurred from aggregate demands during

24          those same hours.

 1               The Commission Should Direct Georgia Power to Create a Robust Bill Comparison Tool

 2   Q143. WHAT STEPS WERE INVOLVED IN YOUR BILL IMPACT ANALYSES PERFORMED IN THIS CASE?

 3   A143. [Mr. Lucas] I had to perform a series of analyses that pulled information from multiple

 4              sources, including Company exhibits, discovery questions, GPC’s webpage, and multiple

 5              tariffs. To calculate the bills of customers on different rates, I created a complex spreadsheet

 6              that calculated bills based on on-peak, off-peak, and super off-peak usage; peak demand

 7              values; fixed customer charges; and ECCR/NCCR/DSM adders, taking care to align different

 8              rates for different TOU periods and months as needed.

 9   Q144. WOULD YOU EXPECT MOST GPC CUSTOMERS TO BE ABLE TO PERFORM A SIMILAR ANALYSIS

10              ON THEIR OWN USAGE?

11   A144. [Mr. Lucas] No. While this is par for the course for a rate design analyst, it is far beyond

12              what could be expected from a typical GPC customer trying to understand their bill. To

13              duplicate these steps, the customer must first set up an account and log in to GPC’s webpage.

14              From there, they must download their hourly usage for at least the past twelve months. They

15              would have to look up the tariff information for each rate they want to analyze, while pulling

16              the appropriate fuel cost recovery rate (of which there are three, plus an Interim Fuel Rider),

17              properly calculating the various adder tariffs (of which there are also three), and then

18              correctly factor in the Municipal Franchise Fee and Local Tax Adjustment. 122

19   Q145. IS IT REASONABLE TO EXPECT CUSTOMERS TO DO THIS?

20   A145. No, it is simply not reasonable to expect customers to be able to perform this analysis even if

21              they were technically able to do so. GPC’s website only has generic information comparing

22              the rates that is not personalized to an individual customers’ usage patterns; just because two

23              customers use 1,000 kWh per month does not mean they will have the same bill on the

24              various TOU rates.


     122
           https://www.georgiapower.com/residential/billing-and-rate-plans/pricing-and-rate-plans.html#tariffs


                                                                                                                 87

 1   Q146. WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND REGARDING THIS ISSUE?

 2   A146. We recommend the Commission require the Company to develop a rate comparison tool that

 3              would automatically perform the analysis above and present a customer with their bill on

 4              each tariff based on their historic usage. The tool could also analyze the customers usage

 5              patterns and suggest more targeted recommendations. For example, if the tool notices that

 6              the customer uses a lot of energy during peak summer hours, it could recommend ways to

 7              save on their bill like using a programmable thermostat to pre-cool their house before 2 PM.

 8                      The Company should also begin collecting peak demand information for customers

 9              who are not on the TOU-RD rate. Although this is not current practice, given the Company

10              has completed its deployment of AMI and has the ability to store this information, it could be

11              an easy enhancement to implement. By collecting this data, the Company would be able to

12              also inform customers how they would fare on the TOU-RD tariff in the bill comparison tool.

 1          Programs,” the user is taken to one final page with links to the Company’s Community Solar

 6   Q149. WHAT DO THE ASTERISKS IN THE FIRST 5 QUESTIONS INDICATE?

 7   A149. These indicate that the question is “for informational purposes only.”

 8   Q150. ARE THESE QUESTIONS REALLY JUST “INFORMATIONAL”?

 9   A150. No. The only result of asking these is to create FUD – fear, uncertainty, and doubt – in the

10          mind of the customer. The answers do not impact the SAT’s results at all. In fact, one can

11          answer anything for the first five questions, including how much sun the roof receives, and

12          not change the results of the analysis.

13                  Many questions and accompanying text are designed to make onsite solar appear less

14          attractive. For example, the third question asks whether your roof is less than 5 years old and

15          suggests “replacing a damaged or failing roof prior to a solar installation.” The clear

16          implication is that roofs over 5 years old are suspect and may not be compatible with a new

17          solar installation. This is simply not true.

18                  Likewise, the questions asking whether the roof has at least 200 square feet of

19          uninterrupted roof space or is shaded have no impact on the results. Most customers would

20          be unaware how much uninterrupted roof space they have or how much shade falls on their

21          house, and they should certainly not climb on their roof to take measurements. Not all roof

22          “interruptions” are the same; normal interruptions such as an exhaust pipe has effectively no

23          impact on pricing, but a customer may be confused into thinking that routine roof installs are

24          “more complex rooftops” that can increase the price. And the only choices on shading are

25          Full Sun / Shaded / Not Sure. The year-round level of shading cannot easily be discerned

26          from any single observation, and shade in the morning may have different impacts on the


                                                                                                          92

 1           performance of the system than shade in the afternoon. None of these nuances are captured

 2           in the SAT.

 3                   Similarly, the question asking whether you plan to live in your house for more than 5

 4           years introduces unneeded doubt and uncertainty. The value of the asset does not vanish if

 5           the original owner moves out of the house, but GPC conspicuously notes that payback

 6           periods may exceed 10 years and that there are costs of removing and re-installing a solar

 7           installation. Modern residential solar systems last for 25 or more years and continue to

 8           produce value for all current and future homeowners. In fact, the solar installation adds value

 9           to the house and can attract a higher selling price, with a recent report noting “We find clear

10           evidence that solar systems are correlated with higher selling prices if those systems are

11           owned by the homeowner.” 125

12   Q151. TURNING TO THE ACTUAL RESULTS OF THE SAT, WHAT DID YOU FIND?

13   A151. The results from a system near Athens appear below in Table 14. To make these figures, the

14           Company was required to make assumptions about the usage patterns of customers installing

15           different system sizes, although this is not disclosed anywhere in the tool’s workflow.

     125
        Begley, Jaclene and Hoen, Ben, Solar at High Noon: Solar Home Premiums in a Rapidly Maturing Market (July
     2, 2021). Available at SSRN: https://ssrn.com/abstract=3894549


                                                                                                               93

 1                       For example, the savings per kW vary depending on the system size and tariff,

 2              meaning that the percentage of Block 3 usage (on the R tariff) and Peak usage (on the TEO-

 3              REO) tariffs are a function of system size. This would only happen if the Company assumed

 4              that customers installing larger systems had higher total usage on the R tariff or a higher on-

 5              peak percentage of usage on the TOU-REO tariff. Although it is not unreasonable to assume

 6              a customer installing a larger system has higher overall usage, there is nothing that prevents a

 7              high-use customer from installing a smaller PV system, particularly given the incentives

 8              embedded in the RNR Instantaneous tariff. Further, there is substantial diversity in on-peak

 9              usage for customers with similar total load levels; using a generic average assumption could

10              have major impacts on the results of this analysis.

11   Q152. WHAT FINANCIAL METRICS ARE ASSUMED IN THE SAT?

12   A152. The SAT assumes that all systems cost $3,600/kW assuming a 30% federal ITC eligibility,

13              although it is unclear and undocumented whether the tool reflects the old 22% ITC or new

14              30% ITC rate. 126 It is also unclear whether the system size and cost is indicative of kWAC or

15              kWDC. The former is used in the Company’s interconnection process, but the latter is often

16              used in conversations between customers and solar installers. The difference in prices

17              between the two assumptions can be roughly 20%, a non-trivial amount. There is also no

18              sourcing for the $3,600/kW figure in the tool.

19   Q153. IS THE “PAYBACK PERIOD” METRIC ALWAYS RELEVANT TO A CUSTOMER?

20   A153. No. The payback period is only relevant to systems purchased with cash. In this case, bill

21              savings gradually offsets the initial net purchase price to produce a time when the purchase

22              price has been fully offset and the “payback period” has been met. But if a customer finances

23              a system, she could realize net positive cash flow on day one. For example, if a customer’s

24              monthly loan fee was a flat $100, and the bill savings from the PV system was $110, she


     126
           Each system cost divided by its capacity divided by 0.7 = $3,600 / kW.


                                                                                                               94

 1           would save $10 immediately. There is no payback period as this metric simply does not

 2           apply to financed purchases.

 3   Q154. WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND WITH REGARD TO THE SAT?

 4   A154. We recommend it be scrapped. The SAT is clearly biased against customer-owned,

 5           customer-sited solar. It requires customers to answer questions that are irrelevant to its

 6           results. The embedded assumptions assume usage patterns and financing methods that are

7            unvetted and non-universal. And even if a customer pushes through all the FUD, the end

8            result is a callback from GPC’s own solar team, not a recommendation to use one of the

9            many qualified installers in the state. Georgia Power customers would be better served if this

10           tool did not exist.


11          The Company Should Explain Why its AMI Data is “Not Precise Enough” for Universal Use

12   Q155. ACCORDING TO COMPANY FILINGS, HOW MANY SMART METERS DOES THE COMPANY HAVE?

13   A155. The Company reported 2.3 million residential and 325,000 non-residential smart meters. 127

14           This represents 100% of residential and industrial customers and 95% of non-residential

15           meters, although interestingly the Company reports 100% of its energy that year being served

16           through AMI meters. 128 The Company appears to have largely completed its AMI rollout by

17           2011. 129

18   Q156. GIVEN THE UNIVERSAL AVAILABILITY OF AMI DATA FROM ITS RESIDENTIAL AND

19           INDUSTRIAL CUSTOMERS, AND NEAR UNIVERSAL AVAILABILITY FROM ITS COMMERCIAL

20           CUSTOMERS, DOES GPC USE ONLY AMI DATA IN ITS COSS AND RATE DESIGN?

21   A156. [Mr. Lucas] Astonishingly, no. Staff noted this discrepancy and asked how it was possible

22           that the Company could not provide peak demand values for individual customers not already

                                                                                                                   95

1               on the TOU-RD tariff but be able to provide hourly data for all customers on other tariffs. 130

2               Staff appears to be reasonably expecting that the Company’s hourly AMI data was

3               aggregated to produce the hourly load profile data for each tariff. I suspect they were as

4               surprised as I was by the Company’s response:
 5                        The rate schedule hourly demands provided in STF-TAI-1-12 are developed from the
 6                        Company’s load research studies as discussed in IRP Technical Appendix Section 9.5
 7                        and in accordance with industry standard practices. The Company’s standard AMI
 8                        meter readings, while programed for the accurate determination of billing
 9                        determinants for rendering bills, are not precise enough to develop individual load
10                        profiles that can be summed to represent a rate schedule’s load nor total system
11                        load...
12
13                        These studies are designed to deliver representative load profiles at the rate level;
14                        they cannot determine load profiles for each individual customer. Therefore, the AMI
15                        meters and readings are appropriately used for billing individual customers and the
16                        Company’s long-standing load research process is appropriately used for developing
17                        the hourly demands for rates. 131
18                        In other words, despite having AMI meters on functionally all of its residential and

19              industrial customers, and on more than 95% of its commercial customers, the Company does

20              not use the AMI data in its COSS or rate design workpapers. Instead, it uses load sampling

21              methods necessary before universal AMI that statistically recreates hourly usage for tariffs

22              and COSS classes from a sample of customers.

23   Q157. WHY IS THIS SO SURPRISING?

24   A157. [Mr. Lucas] Because the only reason to do load research using a sample of load meters is if

25              access to population-level AMI data was not available. There is no sub-hourly data in COSS;

26              everything is aggregated to a full hour. Residential AMI meters provide hourly readings.

27              Certain commercial rate designs bill based on sub-hourly durations, but these can be easily

28              summed to produce hourly values. Tariff groups that use the roughly 5% or 15,000 non-AMI

29              meters in the commercial class may need to use load research, but that represents a tiny

30              fraction of the Company’s total customers and energy usage. If the Company has hourly or


     130
           STF-TAI-4-5.
     131
           Id.


                                                                                                                 96

1             sub-hourly usage from every customer in a tariff or customer group, it can and should just

2             add those values up.

3                      Load research, no matter how good, can only introduce errors when compared to

4             counting an entire population. The Company states in IRP Technical Appendix Section 9.5

5             that about 2,000 customers have had load research meters installed on their property, or less

6             than 0.1% of total customers. While this number of meters almost certainly provides a

7             statistically robust sample, it necessarily will have uncertainty with respect to directly

8             counting the population.

 9   Q158. WHAT IS YOUR RESPONSE TO THE COMPANY’S OBSERVATION THAT AMI METERS ARE

10            ACCURATE ENOUGH FOR BILLING BUT NOT FOR LOAD RESEARCH?

11   A158. It is confusing at best. First, the Company did not provide any context for what it means by

12            “not precise enough”, but precision is typically related to overall accuracy of the meters. For

13            example, there are multiple ANSI standards on meters. Specifically, ANSI standard C.12-

14            2022 defines various accuracy levels of metering equipment, such as Class 0.5 (+/- 0.5% of

15            actual) and Class 0.2 (+/- 0.2% of actual). 132

16                     While certain meters are more accurate than others, and it is possible that the

17            Company installed less accurate meters (e.g., Class 0.5 instead of Class 0.2) in its customer

18            base, the measurement errors found in a population should be randomly distributed and non-

19            correlated. This means that even if individual meters have errors up to 0.5% under or over

20            the actual reading, these errors should cancel each other out as more meters are included in

21            the total. The result of adding up all the values from thousands or millions of meters would

22            be a total that is functionally indistinguishable from the real value. 133

23                     The only way in which this would not hold true is if the errors of the individual

24            meters are not randomly distributed and/or are correlated. For instance, if the meters the

25            Company installed all tended to read higher values than actual, then the results from adding

     132
         The previous ANSI standard for meters when GPC was rolling out AMI was C12.20. This has since been
     merged into a newer standard and updated. https://blog.ansi.org/ansi-c12-1-2022-code-electricity-metering/
     133
         Statistically speaking, the more samples in a data set, the lower the margin of error around the actual value.


                                                                                                                          97

 1              up all of the meters in the population to produce a total could be skewed. Of course, this also

2               would mean that the Company is systematically over-billing customers, which is its own

3               problem.

 3   Q161. WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND ON THIS ISSUE?

 4   A161. [Mr. Lucas] I recommend the Company explain in detail why it is not using its AMI data in

 5           its COSS and rate design for customer classes or tariffs that have universal or near-universal

 6           AMI deployment. Absent statistically valid reasons, the Commission should direct the

 7           Company to use AMI data to its maximum extent in its COSS and rate design going forward

 8           while eliminate as many load research costs as possible. Customers paid for AMI and expect

 9           to get benefits from that investment such as cost savings from load research. Instead, it

10           appears the Company is installing and maintaining duplicative load research meters while

11           also charging customers for Company resources to analyze the data.

12                    The Commission should also request an investigation as to whether the discrepancies

13           between the load research values and the AMI billing values are resulting in the over-

14           collection or under-collection of revenue from certain tariffs.


15                      The Commission Should Approve the 4CP Production Cost Allocator

16   Q162. HOW DOES THE COMPANY ALLOCATE COSTS IN ITS COSS?

17   A162. In the Company’s COSS, production capacity costs are generally allocated to customer

18           classes based on a 12CP method, while transmission costs are generally allocated based on a

19           4CP method. Distribution costs are allocated based on either the 4CP (voltage levels C-E) or

20           Class NCP (voltage levels F-G) demand levels. 137

21   Q163. IS THIS APPROACH TYPICAL IN YOUR EXPERIENCE?

22   A163. [Mr. Lucas] It is somewhat unusual in my experience, particularly for a summer-peaking

23           utility such as the Company. I have usually seen production costs allocated based on a 4CP


     136
         STF-TAI-1-16 Attachment A TRADE SECRET. Value represents the excess collections of base, fuel, and adder
     rider revenue for the R and FlatBill service based on an -xxx-xxx of billed kWh.
     137
         The Company allocates demand-based production costs and step-up substation costs on a 12CP basis, and high-
     voltage transmission costs on an 80% 4CP/ 20% 12CP basis. Lower-voltage transmission assets are allocated on a
     4CP or NCP basis depending on voltage level. Evans Direct at 17.


                                                                                                                 99

 1           method or a combination 4CP and energy method and transmission costs allocated on a 12CP

 2           method. Higher voltage, shared distribution assets are typically allocated based on a Class

 3           NCP basis, while lower voltage assets are sometimes allocated based on Class NCP and

 4           sometimes on the Sum of Individual Maximum Demand (“SIMD”) allocator. 138

 5                    It is clear from the Company’s testimony that other parties have noted that production

 6           costs are often allocated based on 4CP demand. In fact, the Company produced alternative

 7           versions of the COSS that included a 4CP production cost allocator, although it does not

 8           recommend their adoption. 139

 9   Q164. HOW DO THE COMPANY’S LOADS CHANGE THROUGHOUT THE YEAR?

10   A164. [Mr. Lucas] The Company’s peak loads are concentrated during the summer months. Figure

11           15 below shows the average, maximum, and minimum 12CP peak in each month from 2017

12           through 2021. 140 While there are some years in which the winter months had high peaks, the

13           average and minimum winter peaks remain considerably lower than summer months. In fact,

14           January 2018, the most extreme winter month in the past 5 years, still had lower demand than

15           every summer month in 2018 and would have been no higher than the 3rd highest month peak

16           in any year between 2017 and 2021.

 3   Q165. WHY DOES THE COMPANY RECOMMEND CONTINUING THE USE OF THE 12CP ALLOCATOR

 4              FOR PRODUCTION COSTS AND NOT RECOMMEND ADOPTING THE 4CP PRODUCTION COST

 5              ALLOCATOR?

 6   A165. [Mr. Lucas] The Company’s testimony is a bit confusing on this point. It indicates that the

 7              12CP allocator has been used by the Company since 1989, is often used in FERC filings for

 8              wholesale jurisdictional purposes, and has been used by many utilities across the country. 141

 9              While these all may be true, this text applies to the 12CP allocator generically, and not the

10              12CP allocator as applied to production costs.

11                          The Company does offer some testimony on the value of 12CP to “summer and

12              winter reliability planning”, which may refer to production capacity. But even if one were to

13              consider winter and summer peak demands as equally important to reliability (which, based

14              on history, they are not), there is no justification for including the shoulder months in

15              production capacity planning given their substantially lower peak demand levels.


     141
           Evans Direct at 14.


                                                                                                                  101

 1   Q166. DOES THE COMPANY TREAT THE IMPORTANCE OF WINTER LOADS CONSISTENTLY ACROSS

 2              ITS APPLICATION?

 3   A166. No. The Company’s testimony on the importance of winter load in planning is contradicted

 4              by one of its discovery responses. When asked by Staff whether it considered adding a

5               winter TOU rate to its rates to reflect winter peaks on the system, the Company responded

6               “At this time, Georgia Power has not identified a need for a winter peak time of use

7               residential rate. This is due to the fact that the Company continues to forecast its peak loads

8               to occur within the summer months. These summer peaks occur during predictable afternoon

9               hours between the months of June through September, which correspond to existing

10              residential time of use offerings.” 142 The Company continued by noting that winter peaks are

11              less predictable, less frequent, and shorter in duration than summer peaks.

12   Q167. WHY IS THIS AN IMPORTANT CONSIDERATION FOR SOLAR CUSTOMERS?

13   A167. Any discussion of the benefits that solar brings should consider how it performs during peak

14              load hours, and for the Company, peak load hours tend to fall on hot, sunny summer

15              afternoons when solar is generating well. The solar generation is both used onsite to meet

16              load and exported to the grid to meet the neighbor’s load. If production costs are not

17              allocated to customers based on these summer months, then solar generation will appear less

18              valuable than it actually is.

19   Q168. WHAT DO YOU RECOMMEND WITH REGARD TO THE PRODUCTION COST ALLOCATOR?

20   A168. [Mr. Lucas] I recommend the Commission adopt the 4CP allocator for production costs. The

21              Company’s historical data and discovery responses show that it has and will continue to be a

22              summer peaking utility, and in my experience, this is the dominant production cost allocator

23              for summer peaking utilities. Further, the 12CP allocator brings in too many low-demand

24              shoulder months that do not contribute to production costs due to spare capacity on the

25              system.


     142
           STF-TAI-1-31.


                                                                                                             102

1                    The Commission Should Set Aside Funding for Transmission Planning Studies

 2   Q169. ARE ANY OF THE TRANSMISSION PROJECTS REQUESTED BY THE COMPANY IN THIS RATE

 3              CASE ASSOCIATED WITH THE NORTH GEORGIA RELIABILITY AND RESILIENCE ACTION

 4              PLAN?

 5   A169. No. The Company’s response to a Staff discovery request noted that none of the projects in

 6              this rate case are associated with the North Georgia Reliability and Resilience Action Plan

 7              and that Plan is "currently under study and development in conjunction with the Integrated

 8              Transmission System Participants." 143

 9   Q170. IS THAT A PROBLEM?

10   A170. Yes. There is no clear indication in this rate case that the Company has allocated additional

11              costs to pay for the North Georgia Reliability and Resilience Action Plan over the next three

12              years. As Georgia faces retirement limitations in the north, and generation interconnection

13              restrictions in the south, it is extremely important that multiple transmission scenarios be

14              evaluated simultaneously, expeditiously. The proposed rate case does not do an adequate job

15              explaining how the Company intends to plan for the future.

16   Q171. DOES STAFF HAVE DEDICATED FUNDING TO CONDUCT TRANSMISSION PLANNING

17              OVERSIGHT?

18   A171. It does not appear that the Company has allocated any additional funding for Staff to assist

19              with development, review, or oversight of the North Georgia Reliability and Resilience

20              Action Plan, or transmission planning oversight more broadly.

21   Q172. HOW COULD ADDITIONAL FUNDING FOR TRANSMISSION PLANNING FOR THE COMPANY

22              AND/OR STAFF BE HELPFUL?

23   A172. Additional funding for the Company could be used to upgrade software programs that

24              evaluate multiple types of economic congestion, as is done in many other regions of the

25              country. It could also be used to allow Staff to issue a request for proposals to hire an


     143
           STF-PIA-4-23.


                                                                                                               103

 1          independent transmission consultant firm to run scenario-based analyses, evaluate various

 2          benefit metrics, and provide advice and recommendations to the Commission regarding

 3          future transmission plans. An independent transmission consultant firm could look beyond

 4          the current 10-year transmission planning horizon and could have a broader scope of work

 5          developed and led directly by the Commission. This sort of independent analysis is already

 6          done to evaluate RFP bids and was used by the Commission Staff in the IRP evaluating

7           generation expansion but has not yet been done with transmission planning.

8    Q173. WOULD STAFF’S INDEPENDENT TRANSMISSION CONSULTANT REPORT REPLACE THE

 9          COMPANY'S NORTH GEORGIA RELIABILITY AND RESILIENCE ACTION PLAN?

10   A173. No. It would provide the Commission alternative recommendations to compare against the

11          Company's recommendations that may even confirm the Company's plan as the preferred

12          plan. Because transmission development can result in assets that last 40 to 60 years, it would

13          be prudent to get a robust second opinion.


                                                                                                        104

 1   VII.       CONCLUSION

 2   Q174. PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR RECOMMENDATIONS IN THIS CASE.

 3   A174. Our recommendations in this case follow:
 4        • The Company’s proposed $200 interconnection fee should be rejected and its current
 5          $5/kW fee for systems under 250 kW maintained. If the Company is able to demonstrate
 6          that its costs for routine steps such as billing system updates and project review are
 7          reasonable, and that the current fees under-collect interconnection review costs, the
 8          Commission should direct the Company to establish tiered interconnection fees that seek to
 9          minimize costs for systems under 10 kW.
10          •    The Commission should deny the Company’s request to adjust revenue by $1.4 million.
11               The Company did not appropriately account for exported energy in its COSS, and as a
12               result, has overallocated costs to customer classes with RNR projects that is not considered
13               in this figure.
14          •    The Commission should strongly reconsider the width and asymmetry of the currently-
15               approved ROE dead band. Ideally, it would be reduced to +/- 50 basis points, but at a
16               minimum should be returned to the +/- 100 basis points in place prior to 2020.
17          •    The Smart Usage tariff should not be the default rate and should be substantially
18               redesigned with either a peak TOU demand charge collecting a fraction of production and
19               transmission costs or a non-coincident peak charge collecting only low-voltage distribution
20               assets. The Commission should require the Company to validate that the Smart Usage
21               tariff is revenue neutral with respect to the traditional Residential tariff.
22          •    The Commission should require the Company to continue offering the Residential and
23               Nights and Weekends tariffs to new premises. The default rate should be changed to the
24               Nights and Weekends tariff.
25          •    The Commission should require the Company to develop a rate comparison tool that would
26               automatically calculate a residential customer’s bill based on historic usage on the various
27               tariff options. It should also be required to collect billing demand data for each customer.
28          •    The Solar Advisor Tool should be scrapped as it is actively biased against onsite solar.
29          •    The Company should explain why its AMI data is insufficiently precise to use in its rate
30               design and COSS. Absent statistically valid reasons, the Commission should direct the
31               Company to use AMI data to its maximum extent in its COSS and rate design going
32               forward while eliminate as many load research costs as possible.
33          •    The Commission should adopt the 4CP allocator for production costs. The Company’s
34               historical data and discovery responses show that it has and will continue to be a summer
35               peaking utility, and in my experience, this is the dominant production cost allocator for
36               summer peaking utilities.
37          •    The Commission should direct the Company to provide funding for Staff and/or
38               independent consultants to perform additional transmission studies related to North
39               Georgia Reliability and Resilience Action Plan and transmission planning generally.

40   Q175. DOES THIS CONCLUDE YOUR TESTIMONY?

41   A175. Yes, it does.


                                                                                                            105

 1           than $350 per year in a few years. Compared to this, the two dimes per year for the NEM

 2           adjustment is literal pocket change.

 3   Q66.    IS THERE ANOTHER MECHANISM IN THE RATE CASE THAT ALSO EXPOSES CUSTOMERS TO

 4           SIGNIFICANT COST INCREASES?

 5   A66.    Yes. Each rate case, the Commission approves a specific ROE and capital structure.

 6           Currently, the Company enjoys a 10.5% ROE and a 54% 56% equity / 46% 44% debt capital

 7           structure.68 These values feed into the COSS to calculate the total revenue requirement based

 8           on forecasted net plant in service, operating expenses, and taxes. From this revenue

 9           requirement, rates are designed based on forecasted usage to exactly collect the revenue

10           requirement. Of course, the assumptions embedded in the COSS, load forecast, and cost

11           projections will have errors, and the actual revenues collected and costs incurred will not

12           exactly match those in the COSS models. The result is an ROE that differs from the

13           Commission-approved 10.5%.

14                   This is addressed in Georgia through the use of an ROE earning band mechanism.

15           While rates are calculated based on assumptions to produce an ROE of 10.5%, if the

16           Company collects revenue or manages costs to produce an ROE above or below this figure

17           within a certain range (the “dead band”), no additional action is required. Currently, the

18           Company’s Commission-approved ROE is 10.5%, but the Company’s multi-year rate plan is

19           authorized to operate within an ROE dead band of 9.5% to 12%.69

20   Q67.    WHAT WAS THE ORIGIN OF THE ROE DEAD BAND MECHANISM?

21   A67.    The ROE dead band concept has been in place since at least the 2010 rate case settlement. In

22           that case, the ROE dead band of 10.25% to 12.25% was established, with a central approved

23           ROE of 11.15%.70 In the 2013 rate case settlement, which remained in force from 2013

24           through 2018, the Commission approved an ROE dead band of 10% to 12% while dropping
